| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Xi_Jinping_sidebar)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Xi_Jinping_sidebar)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Xi_Jinping_sidebar) |

**Xi Jinping**[^cite_note-7] (born 15 June 1953) is a Chinese politician who is the [paramount leader](262766.md) of [China](5405.md). He has served as the [general secretary of the Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) (CCP) and [chairman of the Party Central Military Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_of_the_Central_Military_Commission_\(China\)) (CMC) since 2012, and as the [president of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_China) and chairman of the State Central Military Commission since 2013.

The elder son of [Xi Zhongxun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Zhongxun)'s second marriage to [Qi Xin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qi_Xin), Xi was born in [Beijing](18603746.md). He is considered a [princeling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeling) but kept a low-key image in his early career. As a teenager, his father was purged, and he was sent down to the rural village of [Liangjiahe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liangjiahe), [Shaanxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaanxi), during the [Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution). He lived in a [yaodong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaodong) there, joined the CCP after several failed attempts, and served as the local [party secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party_Committee_Secretary). After studying [chemical engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_engineering) at [Tsinghua University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsinghua_University) as a [worker-peasant-soldier student](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worker-peasant-soldier_student), Xi rose through the party ranks. He served as [governor of Fujian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Fujian) from 1999 to 2002 before serving as [governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Zhejiang) and [party secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_Secretary_of_Zhejiang) of [Zhejiang](96602.md) from 2002 to 2007. Xi briefly replaced disgraced Shanghai party secretary [Chen Liangyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Liangyu) in 2007, the same year he joined the [Politburo Standing Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politburo_Standing_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) and became the first-ranked member of the [Secretariat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party). Appointed [vice president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_of_China) in 2008 and [vice chairman of the CMC](46959142.md) in 2010, he succeeded [Hu Jintao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Jintao) as China's leader in 2012, becoming the first paramount leader to be born after the [foundation of the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China).

Under his leadership, China adopted the [Made in China 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Made_in_China_2025) policy and promoted higher-value manufacturing and efforts to reduce reliance on foreign technology. His tenure has also been characterized by a sweeping [anti-corruption campaign](45655698.md), a [restructuring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deepening_National_Defense_and_Military_Reform) of the [People's Liberation Army](66890.md), [poverty alleviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_against_poverty), increased state control over the economy and society, and regulatory crackdowns on the private sector, notably in real estate, technology, and for-profit tutoring, alongside expanded [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_China) and [mass surveillance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_surveillance_in_China) and a deterioration in [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_China). During the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_mainland_China), he imposed [zero-COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero-COVID) policies for two years before lifting them following widespread [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_COVID-19_protests_in_China).

&#x20;Xi has pursued an assertive foreign policy, including toward [China–United States relations](277880.md), in the [South China Sea](74209.md), and along the [Sino-Indian border dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_border_dispute). He expanded China's economic and diplomatic presence in Africa and Eurasia through the [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative). His [2015 meeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_meeting_of_Ma_Ying-jeou_and_Xi_Jinping) with Taiwanese president [Ma Ying-jeou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Ying-jeou), the first between [cross-strait](2596399.md) leaders since the [Chinese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Civil_War), was followed by deteriorating relations under the [Democratic Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party). He oversaw the enactment of the [2020 Hong Kong national security law](64033197.md), which effectively suppressed the city's [pro-democracy camp](3291227.md). In 2018, Xi amended the [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_China) to [remove presidential term limits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_amendment_to_the_Constitution_of_China) and, upon his re-election in 2023, became the first Chinese president to serve more than two terms. Xi's ideology, known as [Xi Jinping Thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought), has been incorporated into the [party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) and national constitutions.&#x20;

## Early life and education

Main article: [Early life of Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_life_of_Xi_Jinping)

Xi Jinping was born on 15 June 1953 in [Beijing](18603746.md) as the third child of [Xi Zhongxun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Zhongxun) and his second wife [Qi Xin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qi_Xin).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong202319–20-8] A [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP) revolutionary, Xi's father held a series of posts after the [founding of the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) in 1949,[^cite_note-\:11-9] including [head of the Party Publicity Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_the_Publicity_Department_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), [vice premier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_Premier_of_China), and [vice chairperson of the Standing Committee of the National People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_Chairperson_of_the_Standing_Committee_of_the_National_People's_Congress).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong202327-10][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorigian2025265-11] Xi has two older sisters, [Qi Qiaoqiao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qi_Qiaoqiao), born in 1949, and Qi An'an (齐安安), born in 1952.[^cite_note-\:13-12] Xi's father was from [Fuping County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuping_County,_Shaanxi), Shaanxi.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorigian202518–19-13]

Xi went to [Beijing Bayi School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Bayi_School), where students were primarily the children of high-ranking military officials.[^cite_note-14] In 1963, when Xi was ten years old, his father was purged from the CCP and sent to work in a factory in [Luoyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luoyang), Henan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorigian202518–19-13] Following the start of the [Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution) in 1966, Xi was persecuted due to his father's status.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202244-15] Student militants ransacked the Xi family home.[^cite_note-Buckley-2015-16] His mother was forced to publicly denounce his father, as he was paraded before a crowd as an enemy of the revolution.[^cite_note-newyorker2-17] After the Bayi School closed down in 1967, Xi was transferred to the [Beijing No. 25 School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_No._25_Middle_School).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorigian2025197-18] In 1968, when Xi was aged 15, his father was imprisoned. Xi left Beijing and arrived in [Liangjiahe Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liangjiahe), [Yan'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yan'an), Shaanxi in the [Down to the Countryside Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Down_to_the_Countryside_Movement).[^cite_note-Page-2020-19] At the time, Yan'an was one of the poorest places in the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202248-20] After a few months, unable to stand rural life, he ran away to Beijing. He was arrested during a crackdown on deserters from the countryside and sent to a work camp to dig ditches. He later returned to the village under the persuasion of his aunt Qi Yun and uncle Wei Zhenwu.[^cite_note-x837-21][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorigian2025199–200-22]



Items used by Xi during his time working in the countryside in [Liangjiahe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liangjiahe)

He then spent a total of seven years in Liangjiahe, where he lived in a [yaodong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaodong), a type of cave house found in northern China.[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24] During his time in Liangjiahe, Xi's half-sister Heping hanged herself at her military academy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorigian2025238–239-25] In 1973, [Yanchuan County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanchuan_County) assigned Xi to Zhaojiahe Village in the Jiajianping Commune to lead social education efforts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESino_United_Electronic_Publishing_Limited202119-26] Due to his effective work and strong rapport with the villagers, the community expressed a desire to keep him. However, after Liangjiahe Village advocated for his return, Xi was sent back there.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202244-15] The village branch secretaries supported his application to the CCP. Yet, due to his father Xi Zhongxun still facing political persecution, the application was initially denied by the higher authorities.[^cite_note-newyorker2-17] According to one account, Xi applied for CCP membership twice; his first application was rejected due to his father's status, but the second time, due to a new policy of not judging offsprings due to their parents, the application was approved.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202255-27] Around that time, as the Liangjiahe village underwent leadership changes, Xi was recommended to become the Party branch chairman of the Liangjiahe Brigade, later taking office.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202234-28] In 1975, when the Yan'an region was allocated two spots at [Tsinghua University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsinghua_University), Xi applied to the university in all three of his allowable choices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202258-29] From 1975 to 1979, Xi studied [chemical engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_engineering) at Tsinghua University as a [worker-peasant-soldier student](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worker-Peasant-Soldier_student) in Beijing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhongZhang20223-30]

## Early political career

Main article: [Early political career of Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_political_career_of_Xi_Jinping)

### Central Military Commission

After graduating in April 1979, Xi worked at the [General Office of the State Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Office_of_the_State_Council) as one of the three secretaries of [Geng Biao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geng_Biao), who was the secretary-general of the [Central Military Commission](214345.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202245-31][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuo2019363-32][^cite_note-newyorker2-17] As part of his job, Xi participated in central meetings and decision-making, dealt with regional and foreign affairs, and became familiar with the handling of central documents, including confidential ones. Xi worked to inspect the armed forces, draft documents, and oversee Hong Kong, Macau, and Taiwan affairs together with Geng.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202245-31]

### Hebei

In March 1982, Xi was appointed deputy party secretary of [Zhengding County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhengding_County) in Hebei.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi2016333-33][^cite_note-34] Together with Lü Yulan, the other deputy party secretary of Zhengding, Xi wrote a letter to the central government addressing the excessive requisitions that burdened local farmers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202250-35][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEXiamen_University_Press201859-36] Their efforts successfully convinced the central government to reduce the annual requisition amount by 14 million kilograms. Xi also set up a pilot project for the [household responsibility system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Household_responsibility_system) in one of the poorer communes despite reservations from some in the local leadership.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan202250-35]

As the secretary of the CCP Zhengding County Committee in July 1983,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChina_City_Press199273–548-37][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEXiamen_University_Press201859-36] Xi initiated several development projects, including the development of "Nine Articles of Zhengding talents",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChina_City_Press199273–548-37] the construction of Changshan Park,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeople's_Literature_Publishing_House198662-38] the restoration of the [Longxing Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longxing_Temple), the formation of a tourism company, and the establishment of the Rongguo Mansion and Zhengding Table Tennis Base.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESocial_Sciences_Literature_Press20233-39] He also persuaded the China Teleplay Production Center to set the filming base of *[Dream of the Red Chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dream_of_the_Red_Chamber_\(1987_TV_series\))* in Zhengding and secured 3.5 million yuan to build Rongguo Mansion,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChengdu_Publishing_House1996286-40] which significantly boosted the county's tourism industry, generating 17.61 million yuan in revenue that year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeijing_Yanshan_Publishing_House1993363-41] Additionally, Xi invited prominent figures such as [Hua Luogeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hua_Luogeng), [Yu Guangyuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yu_Guangyuan), Pan Chengxiao to visit Zhengding,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHebei_Youth_Magazine19845-42] which eventually led to the development of the county's "semi-urban" strategy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESocial_Sciences_Literature_Press20233-39] leveraging its proximity to Shijiazhuang for diverse business growth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeijing_Book2018141-43][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeking_University_Press201717-44]

In September 1984, during a briefing session chaired by He Zai, the secretary-general of the [CCP Central Organization Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_Department_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), Xi's strategic vision and comprehensive understanding of Zhengding County's development were highlighted.[^cite_note-d652-45] He Zai, along with [Wei Jianxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wei_Jianxing), deputy head of the CCP Central Organization Department, communicated these findings to [Hu Yaobang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Yaobang), describing Xi as a leader with a strategic outlook and a strong alliance ideology between workers and peasants.[^cite_note-k053-46][^cite_note-p946-47] In 1985, Xi participated in a study tour on corn processing and traveled to [Iowa](26810748.md), the United States,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAoyagiShurtleff2017262-48] to study agricultural production and corn processing technology.[^cite_note-o756-49][^cite_note-d652-45] During his visit to the U.S., the CCP Central Organization Department decided to transfer him to [Xiamen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiamen) as a member of the Standing Committee of the CCP Xiamen Municipal Committee and as vice mayor.[^cite_note-k053-46]

### Fujian

Arriving in Xiamen as vice-mayor in June 1985, Xi drafted the first strategic plan for the development of the city, the Xiamen Economic and Social Development Strategy for 1985–2000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuangZhang20169-50] From August, he oversaw the resolution for Yundang Lake's comprehensive treatments,[^cite_note-b381-51] and prepared [Xiamen Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiamen_Airlines),[^cite_note-m820-52] the Xiamen Economic Information Center,[^cite_note-g215-53] and the Xiamen Special Administrative Region Road Project. He then married [Peng Liyuan](10462776.md) in Xiamen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuhn201142-54][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDillon202193-55] In September 1988, he was appointed as the secretary of [Ningde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ningde) before being elevated to head of the region.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown20219-56] Ningde's economy was far worse at that time than that of Fuzhou and Xiamen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown20226-57] Xi condensed his work log and memories of his Ningde period into the book *Getting out of Poverty*,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuazhong_University_of_Science_and_Technology_Press202174-58] and led local poverty eradicating efforts and local CCP building projects.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeng202311-59] The CCP Fujian Provincial Committee decided in May 1990 to assign Xi to [Fuzhou City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_City) as the Municipal Committee Secretary.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELam202321-60] In 1995, Xi Jinping was elevated to deputy secretary of the [Fujian Provincial Committee of the Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujian_Provincial_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamel_Bells_Magazine200045-61]

In September 1997, he was elected as an alternate member of the [15th CCP Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) by the [15th Party National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party). In 1998, he became a member of the [9th National People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_National_People's_Congress).[^cite_note-62] In 1999, he was promoted to the office of Vice Governor of Fujian, and became governor a year later. Xi proposed the concept of the Golden Triangle at Min River and oversaw the construction of the Fuzhou 3820 Project Master Plan,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan2022547-63] which outlines Fuzhou's growth strategy for 3, 8, and 20 years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFuzhou_Yearbook199516-64] He also championed the development of [Fuzhou Changle International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_Changle_International_Airport),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan2022101-65] the Min River Water Transfer Project, the Fuzhou Telecommunication Hub, and [Fuzhou Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuzhou_Port), among others. He also focused on attracting Taiwanese and foreign investment,[^cite_note-66] establishing Southwest TPV Electronics and Southeast Automobile in Fuzhou, and fostering [Fuyao Glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuyao_Glass), [Newland Digital Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newland_Digital_Technology) and other manufacturing firms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELam202321-60] He served as [governor of Fujian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Fujian) from 1999 to 2002, during which he presented the notion of "[Megalopolises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalopolises_in_China)" and advocated for the inter-island growth strategy of Fuzhou and Xiamen, which motivated local officials to swiftly overcome the repercussions of the [Yuanhua smuggling case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lai_Changxing) ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language): 远华走私案) and adopt a new development strategy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECamel_Bells_Magazine200045-61] Xi also oversaw the development of "Digital Fujian", including the province's complaint hotline into the "[12345 Citizen Service Platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/12345_hotline)", so enhancing organizational efficiency.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan2022547-63]

### Zhejiang

In October 2002, Xi left Fujian and became the deputy governor, acting governor and deputy Party secretary of neighbouring Zhejiang. He eventually took over as [provincial Party Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujian_Provincial_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) secretary after the [16th Party National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), occupying a top provincial office for the first time in his career.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan2022134-67] In November 2002, he was elected a full member of the [16th Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) by the 16th Party National Congress, marking his ascension to the national stage. While in Zhejiang, Xi presided over reported growth rates averaging 14% per year.[^cite_note-68] During this period, Zhejiang increasingly transitioned away from heavy industry.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChatwin2024121-69] Xi's career in Zhejiang was marked by a tough and straightforward stance against corrupt officials. This earned him a name in the national media and drew the attention of China's top leaders.[^cite_note-70] Between 2004 and 2007, [Li Qiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Qiang) acted as Xi's chief of staff through his position as secretary-general of the [Zhejiang Party Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhejiang_Provincial_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), where they developed close mutual ties.[^cite_note-71] During this period, Xi and Li drafted the [Double Eight Strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_Eight_Strategy), which listed eight comparative advantages of Zhejiang and eight corresponding actions to improve the province.[^cite_note-72]

### Shanghai

Following the dismissal of [Shanghai Party secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_Secretary_of_Shanghai) [Chen Liangyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Liangyu) in September 2006 due to a [social security fund scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_pension_scandal), Xi was transferred to Shanghai on 24 March 2007, where he was the party secretary there for seven months.[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74] While in Shanghai, he worked on preserving unity of the local party organisation. He pledged there would be no purges during his administration, despite the fact many local officials were thought to have been implicated in the Chen Liangyu corruption scandal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELam201556-75] In Shanghai, Xi was known for supporting market based reforms.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77] On most issues, Xi largely echoed the line of the central leadership.[^cite_note-78] He developed a reputation in his early career for avoiding controversy and not making political opponents.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorst202575-79]

### Vice presidency



Xi greeting U.S. president [George W. Bush](3414021.md) in August 2008

Immediately after the [17th Party Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/17th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), Xi was elected by the first plenary session of the [17th Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/17th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) to be the 6th-ranking member of the [Politburo Standing Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politburo_Standing_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) on 22 October 2007. In addition, Xi became a member of the [17th Secretariat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/17th_Secretariat_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party). He also led the [Central Leading Group for Party Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Leading_Group_for_Party_Building) and the [Central Hong Kong and Macau Affairs Leading Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Leading_Group_on_Hong_Kong_and_Macau_Affairs), as well as being the deputy leader of the [Central Foreign Affairs Leading Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Foreign_Affairs_Commission).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan2022198-80] He was ranked above [Li Keqiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Keqiang) in the PSC, an indication that he was going to succeed [Hu Jintao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Jintao) as China's [next leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_of_power_in_China). This assessment was further supported at the [first session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_session_of_the_11th_National_People's_Congress) of the [11th National People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11th_National_People's_Congress) in March 2008, when Xi was elected as [vice president of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_of_China).[^cite_note-81]

Following his elevation, Xi held a broad range of portfolios. He was put in charge of the comprehensive preparations for the [2008 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Summer_Olympics) in Beijing,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEXu2026189-82] as well as being the central government's leading figure in Hong Kong and Macau affairs. In addition, he also became the [president of the Central Party School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Central_Party_School), the CCP's cadre-training and ideological education school. In the wake of the [2008 Sichuan earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Sichuan_earthquake), Xi visited [Shaanxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaanxi), which was also hit by the earthquake.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan2022214-83] After the Olympics, Xi was assigned the post of committee chair for the preparations of celebrations for the [60th anniversary of the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60th_anniversary_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China). He was also reportedly at the helm of a top-level CCP committee dubbed the [6521 Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6521_Project), which was charged with ensuring social stability during a series of politically sensitive anniversaries in 2009.[^cite_note-84] Following the [July 2009 Ürümqi riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2009_%C3%9Cr%C3%BCmqi_riots), Xi was put in charge to restore order, aided by security chief [Zhou Yongkang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Yongkang).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChan2022216-85] Xi also took active part in diplomacy, visiting various countries as vice president.[^cite_note-86]



Xi with Russian president [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev) on 28 September 2010

Xi's position as the apparent successor to become the paramount leader was threatened with the rapid rise of [Bo Xilai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bo_Xilai), the [party secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_Secretary_of_Chongqing) of [Chongqing](171752.md) at the time. Bo was expected to join the PSC after the [18th Party Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), with most expecting that he would try to eventually maneuver himself into replacing Xi.[^cite_note-87] Bo's policies in Chongqing inspired imitations throughout China and received praise from Xi himself during Xi's visit to Chongqing in 2010. Records of praises from Xi were later erased after he became paramount leader. Bo's downfall would come with the [Wang Lijun incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Lijun_incident), which opened the door for Xi to come to power without challengers.[^cite_note-88] On 18 October 2010, at the fifth plenary session of the [17th Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/17th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), Xi was elected to be a vice chairman of the CCP Central Military Commission,[^cite_note-89] followed by his appointment as vice chairman of the state CMC on 28 October by the [Standing Committee of the National People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_Committee_of_the_National_People's_Congress).[^cite_note-90]

A few months before his ascendancy to the party leadership, Xi disappeared from official media coverage and cancelled meetings with foreign officials for several weeks beginning on 1 September 2012, causing rumors.[^cite_note-newyorker2-17] He then reappeared on 15 September.[^cite_note-91] On 15 November 2012, immediately after the [18th Party National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), Xi was elected by the first plenary session the [18th Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) to the posts of Party general secretary and chairman of the Party CMC. This made him both the [CCP leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) and, informally, China's [paramount leader](262766.md). The following day Xi led the new line-up of the PSC onto the stage in their first public appearance.[^cite_note-BBCconfirm2-92] The membership of the PSC was reduced from nine to seven, with Xi and Li Keqiang retaining their seats; the other five members were new.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95] In December 2012, Xi [visited Guangdong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping's_southern_tour) in his first trip outside Beijing since taking the general secretaryship. The overarching theme of the trip was to call for further economic reform and a strengthened military.[^cite_note-96] Xi was elected president on 14 March 2013, in a confirmation vote during the [first session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_session_of_the_12th_National_People's_Congress) of the [12th National People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/12th_National_People's_Congress) in Beijing. He received 2,952 for, one vote against, and three abstentions.[^cite_note-BBCconfirm2-92] He replaced [Hu Jintao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Jintao), who retired after serving two terms.[^cite_note-97] Xi was also elected as the chairman of the state CMC. He subsequently nominated Li Keqiang to be the premier, who was then appointed by the NPC.[^cite_note-bbc-98]

## Leadership

Main article: [General secretaryship of Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_secretaryship_of_Xi_Jinping)

### Political developments

See also: [Xi Jinping faction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_faction)

Political observers have called Xi the most powerful Chinese leader since Chairman [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong), especially since the ending of presidential two-term limits in 2018.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101] Xi has departed from the CCP's previous [collective leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_leadership) practices.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaranovitch2021249–265-103] Observers have said that Xi has seriously diluted the influence of the once-dominant [Tuanpai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuanpai), also called the Youth League Faction, which were CCP officials who rose through the Communist Youth League (CYLC).[^cite_note-104] Xi has had a [cult of personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_of_personality) constructed around himself since entering office[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] with books, cartoons, pop songs and dance routines honouring his rule.[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-PhilGuard12-108] [Xi's cult of personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping's_cult_of_personality) has been especially pronounced in Xinjiang.[^cite_note-109] In 2016, the [sixth plenary session of the 18th Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_plenary_session_of_the_18th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) officially announced Xi as the [leadership core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leadership_core), making him the fourth leader to have been given this designation after Mao Zedong, [Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping) and [Jiang Zemin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Zemin); [Hu Jintao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Jintao) was never given this designation.[^cite_note-guardian2-110]

Xi and other members of the new [20th Politburo Standing Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Politburo_Standing_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) meeting the press following Xi's re-election as general secretary in October 2022

Xi was re-elected as general secretary and CMC chairman by the [19th Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/19th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) after the [19th Party National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/19th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), held between 18 and 24 October 2017; the [19th Politburo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/19th_Politburo_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) was noted for containing no potential successor.[^cite_note-111] In March 2018, the [first session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_session_of_the_13th_National_People's_Congress) of the [13th National People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/13th_National_People's_Congress) passed [constitutional amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_amendment_to_the_Constitution_of_China) including removal of term limits for the president and vice president, the creation of a National Supervisory Commission, as well as codifying the leading role of the CCP.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] Xi was reappointed as president, now without term limits,[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115] while Li Keqiang was reappointed premier.[^cite_note-116] In its sixth plenary session in November 2021, the 19th Central Committee adopted the *[Resolution on the Major Achievements and Historical Experience of the Party over the Past Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resolution_on_the_Major_Achievements_and_Historical_Experience_of_the_Party_over_the_Past_Century)*, which evaluated the party's historical legacy from its [foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) in 1921 until its [100th anniversary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100th_Anniversary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) in 2021. The third historical resolution after the ones adopted by Mao and Deng,[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118] Xi's one did not herald a major change in how the CCP evaluated its history in comparison.[^cite_note-119] To accompany the resolution, the CCP promoted the terms [Two Establishments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Establishments) and [Two Upholds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Upholds), calling the CCP to unite around and protect Xi's core status within the party.[^cite_note-120]

The [20th Party National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), held between 16 and 22 October 2022, has overseen amendments in the [CCP constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), with the overall result of the Congress further strengthening Xi's power.[^cite_note-121] Xi was subsequently re-elected as general secretary of the CCP and chairman of the CMC for a third term during the [first plenary session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_plenary_session_of_the_20th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) of the [20th Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) held on 23 October 2022, held immediately after the Party Congress. Xi's re-election made him the first party leader since Mao Zedong to be chosen for a third term.[^cite_note-122] The new Politburo Standing Committee elected by the Central Committee was filled almost completely with people close to Xi, with four out of the seven members of the previous PSC stepping down.[^cite_note-123] Xi was unanimously re-elected as the president and chairman of the PRC Central Military Commission on 10 March 2023 during the [first session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_session_of_the_14th_National_People's_Congress) of the [14th National People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_National_People's_Congress).[^cite_note-124] At the same time, Xi ally [Li Qiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Qiang) succeeded Li Keqiang as the Premier.[^cite_note-125]

### Anti-corruption campaign

Main article: [Anti-corruption campaign under Xi Jinping](45655698.md)

> "To speak the truth" means to focus on the nature of things, to speak frankly, and follow the truth. This is an important embodiment of a leading official's characteristics of truth seeking, embodying justice, devotion to public interests, and uprightness. Moreover, he highlighted that the premise of telling the truth is to listen to the truth.

— Xi Jinping during a speech in 2012

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeople's_Daily20193–39-126]

Xi called for a crackdown on corruption immediately after he ascended to power, starting an [anti-corruption campaign](45655698.md). In his inaugural speech as general secretary, Xi mentioned that fighting corruption was one of the toughest challenges for the party.[^cite_note-127] A few months into his term, Xi outlined the [Eight-point Regulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eight-point_Regulation), listing rules intended to curb corruption and waste during official party business; it aimed at stricter discipline on the conduct of officials. Xi vowed to root out "tigers and flies", that is, high-ranking officials and ordinary party functionaries.[^cite_note-128] He also launched the [Party's Mass Line Education and Practice Activities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party's_Mass_Line_Education_and_Practice_Activities), aiming CCP cadres to use [mass line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_line) and instill Party discipline. The campaign lasted from 2013 to 2014.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFangLi202495-129]

Xi's anti-corruption campaign has led to the downfall of prominent incumbent and retired CCP officials, including members of the PSC.[^cite_note-130] Xi initiated cases against former CMC vice chairmen [Xu Caihou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xu_Caihou) and [Guo Boxiong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guo_Boxiong), former PSC member and security chief [Zhou Yongkang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Yongkang) and former CCP General Office director [Ling Jihua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ling_Jihua).[^cite_note-131] Xi has overseen significant reforms of the [Central Commission for Discipline Inspection](1526134.md) (CCDI), CCP's highest [internal control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_control) institution.[^cite_note-xinj-132] He and CCDI Secretary [Wang Qishan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Qishan) further institutionalized CCDI's independence from the day-to-day operations of the CCP, improving its ability to function as a *bona fide* control body.[^cite_note-xinj-132] Along with Wang Qishan, Xi's administration spearheaded the formation of "centrally-dispatched inspection teams". These were cross-jurisdictional squads whose task was to gain an understanding of the operations of provincial and local party organizations and enforce party discipline mandated by Beijing. Work teams had the effect of identifying and initiating investigations of high-ranking officials. Over one hundred provincial-ministerial level officials were implicated during a nationwide anti-corruption campaign. These included former and current regional officials, leading figures of state-owned enterprises and central government organs, and generals. Within the first two years of the campaign alone, over 200,000 officials received warnings, fines, and demotions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeilmann201762–75-133] As of 2023, approximately 2.3 million government officials have been prosecuted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJin2023129-134]

According to *The Wall Street Journal*, anti-corruption punishment to officials at or above the vice ministerial level need approval from Xi.[^cite_note-Chin-2021-135] In 2017, the campaign led to the downfall of Chongqing Party Secretary and Politburo member [Sun Zhengcai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_Zhengcai).[^cite_note-136] In March 2018, the [National Supervisory Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Supervisory_Commission) was established as the highest state supervisory and anti-corruption authority in an effort to aid the CCDI.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung202457-137] In January 2018, Xi launched a three-year [Special Campaign to Crack Down on Organized Crime and Eliminate Evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Campaign_to_Crack_Down_on_Organized_Crime_and_Eliminate_Evil) that lasted until 2020.[^cite_note-\:04-138] After the special campaign exposed problems in the legal system, the CCP announced a campaign to [educate and rectify the political and legal teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_and_Rectification_of_the_Political_and_Legal_Teams) in July 2020.[^cite_note-\:022-139][^cite_note-140] Especially since 2023, Xi has also overseen significant anti-corruption efforts in the military, with some targets involving those that rose to prominence under his leadership.[^cite_note-141] Those targeted included former defense ministers [Li Shangfu](55799907.md) and [Wei Fenghe](38755987.md),[^cite_note-142] CMC vice chairmen [He Weidong](62571923.md) and [Zhang Youxia](17853681.md), and CMC member [Miao Hua](47605690.md).[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144] In addition, former Xinjiang Party Secretary and Politburo member [Ma Xingrui](46270468.md) was put under investigation in 2026.[^cite_note-145] [Bloomberg News](3057255.md) estimated that as October 2025, at least 14 generals out of 79 appointed under Xi's leadership have been ousted.[^cite_note-146] Xi has introduced stringent restrictions on [naked officials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naked_official), CCP officials with spouses or children residing abroad, eventually culminating in a 2025 campaign to require cadres of vice-ministerial rank or above being required to either repatriate family members or resign from office.[^cite_note-\:09-147]

### Media and censorship

Main articles: [Censorship in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_China) and [Internet censorship in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_censorship_in_China)

Since Xi became general secretary, censorship has stepped up.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-\:16-149] Xi has overseen the increased coordination and consolidation of censorship authorities, raising their efficiency, and under his leadership censorship practices have tightened.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirk201822–24-150] Xi has called on for more [positive energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positive_energy_\(China\)) at Chinese media, referring to the need for uplifting messages as opposed to critical or negative ones.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYangTang20181–22-151] In August 2013, he gave what became known as the [August 19 speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_19_speech), emphasizing the guiding role of ideology in propaganda.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudd202486-152] At the [Symposium on News Reporting and Public Opinion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symposium_on_News_Reporting_and_Public_Opinion) in 2016, Xi stated that "[party and government-owned media must hold the family name of the party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_media_takes_the_party's_last_name)" and that the state media "must embody the party's will, safeguard the party's authority".[^cite_note-153]

Xi's administration has overseen more Internet restrictions imposed, and is described as being "stricter across the board" on speech than previous administrations.[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-\:16-149] In 2013, the State Internet Information Office summoned influential bloggers to a seminar to instruct them to avoid writing about politics, the CCP, or making statements contradicting official narratives. Many bloggers stopped writing about controversial topics, and [Weibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weibo) went into decline, with much of its readership shifting to [WeChat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WeChat) users speaking to limited social circles.[^cite_note-Grigg2-155] The SIIO also formulated the [Seven Bottom Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Bottom_Lines), which serve as the bottom lines that internet users and social media companies in China must adhere to.[^cite_note-\:011-156] In September 2013, the [Supreme People's Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_People's_Court) authorized a three-year prison term for bloggers who shared more than 500 times any content considered "defamatory".[^cite_note-Grigg2-155]

In 2014, the Chinese government launched the [Cleaning the Web](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleaning_the_Web_2014) campaign, aiming to crack down on pornographic, vulgar, and politically questionable content.[^cite_note-157] It also launched [Operation Qinglang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Qinglang) campaigns since 2016 to "clean up" the Chinese internet.[^cite_note-158] China under Xi has taken a strong stand to control internet usage inside China, including [Google](1092923.md), [Facebook](7529378.md)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBougon2018157–65-159] and [Wikipedia](5043734.md),[^cite_note-160] advocating Internet censorship under the concept of [internet sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_sovereignty).[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] Likewise, the situation for users of Weibo has been described as a change from fearing one's account would be deleted, to fear of arrest.[^cite_note-163] The [Provisions on the Governance of the Online Information Content Ecosystem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisions_on_the_Governance_of_the_Online_Information_Content_Ecosystem), which came into effect in 2020, defined the scope of legal expression for service platforms and content creators.[^cite_note-164] Under Xi's administration, China started enforcing an [Internet real-name system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_real-name_system_in_China) for online platforms, requiring them to collect users' real names, ID numbers, and other information when providing services.[^cite_note-165]

### Reforms

In November 2013, at the conclusion of the [third plenum of the 18th Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_plenary_session_of_the_18th_Central_Committee_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), the Communist Party adopted the *[Decision on Several Major Issues Concerning Comprehensively Deepening Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decision_on_Several_Major_Issues_Concerning_Comprehensively_Deepening_Reform)*, a far-reaching reform agenda that alluded to changes in both economic and social policy. Xi signaled at the plenum that he was consolidating control of the massive internal security organization that was formerly the domain of Zhou Yongkang.[^cite_note-brook2-166] The [Central Leading Group for Comprehensively Deepening Reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Leading_Group_for_Comprehensively_Deepening_Reforms)—another *ad hoc* policy coordination body led by Xi upgraded to a commission in 2018—was also formed to oversee the implementation of the reform agenda.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] Termed "comprehensive deepening reforms", they were said to be the most significant since Deng Xiaoping's 1992 Southern Tour. The plenum also announced economic reforms and resolved to abolish the *[laogai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laogai)* system of "[re-education through labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Re-education_through_labor)", which was largely seen as a blot on China's human rights record. The system has faced significant criticism for years from domestic critics and foreign observers.[^cite_note-brook2-166]

In 2015, Xi announced the [Four Comprehensives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Comprehensives), namely comprehensively build a [moderately prosperous society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderately_prosperous_society), deepening reform, governing the nation according to [law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_law), and strictly governing the Party;[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung202423-170] in 2021, at the 100th anniversary of the CCP, Xi declared that China achieved its goal of building a moderately prosperous society in all respects.[^cite_note-171] In January 2016, a [two-child policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-child_policy) replaced the [one-child policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-child_policy),[^cite_note-172] which was in turn was replaced with a [three-child policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-child_policy) in May 2021.[^cite_note-173] In July 2021, all family size limits as well as penalties for exceeding them were removed.[^cite_note-174] Since abandoning population control restrictions, China has pursued [pro-natalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natalism) policies in an attempt to boost the birth rate.[^cite_note-175]

#### Political reforms

See also: [Neoauthoritarianism (China)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoauthoritarianism_\(China\)) and [Xi Jinping Thought on Party Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought_on_Party_Building)

> The most essential feature of socialism with Chinese characteristics is the leadership of the Communist Party of China. The greatest advantage of the socialist system with Chinese characteristics is the leadership of the Communist Party of China, and the party is the highest political leading force.

— Xi Jinping during a speech in June 2018

[^cite_note-176]

Xi's administration enacted a number of changes to the structure of the CCP and state bodies, especially in a large overhaul in 2018. These reforms have been characterized by the integration of CCP and state bodies.[^cite_note-177] Beginning in 2013, the CCP under Xi has created a series of Central Leading Groups: supra-ministerial steering committees, designed to bypass existing institutions when making decisions, and ostensibly make policy-making a more efficient process. Xi was also believed to have diluted the authority of premier Li Keqiang, taking authority over the economy which has generally been considered to be the domain of the premier.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] February 2014 oversaw the creation of the [Central Leading Group for Cybersecurity and Informatization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Cyberspace_Affairs_Commission) with Xi as its leader. The State Internet Information Office (SIIO), previously under the [State Council Information Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Council_Information_Office) (SCIO), was transferred to the central leading group and renamed in English into the Cyberspace Administration of China.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAttrillFritz20214-180] As part of managing the financial system, the [Financial Stability and Development Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_Stability_and_Development_Committee), a State Council body, was established in 2017. Chaired by vice premier [Liu He](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_He_\(politician\)) during its existence, the committee was disestablished by the newly established Central Financial Commission during the 2023 Party and state reforms.[^cite_note-181] Xi has increased the role of the [Central Financial and Economic Affairs Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Financial_and_Economic_Affairs_Commission) at the expense of the [State Council](44498.md).[^cite_note-182]

2018 has seen the [deepening the reform of the Party and state institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deepening_the_reform_of_the_Party_and_state_institutions). In that year, several [central leading groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leading_Small_Group) including [reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Comprehensively_Deepening_Reforms_Commission), cyberspace affairs, [finance and economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Financial_and_Economic_Affairs_Commission), and [foreign affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Foreign_Affairs_Commission) were upgraded to commissions.[^cite_note-Buckley-2018-183] The powers of the Central Publicity Department was strengthened, which now oversaw the newly established [China Media Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Media_Group) (CMG).[^cite_note-Buckley-2018-183] Two State Council departments. one dealing with [overseas Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Chinese_Affairs_Office), and other one dealing with [religious affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Administration_for_Religious_Affairs), were merged into the [United Front Work Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_Work_Department) of the CCP while another commission dealing with [ethnic affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Ethnic_Affairs_Commission) was brought under formal UFWD leadership.[^cite_note-Buckley-2018-183] In 2020, all elections at all levels of the [people's congress system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System_of_people's_congress) and NPC were mandated to adhere to the leadership of the CCP.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaoKe2023159–184-184] 2023 has seen further reforms to the CCP and state bureaucracy called the [plan on reforming Party and state institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plan_on_reforming_Party_and_state_institutions), which included the strengthening of Party control over the financial and technology domains.[^cite_note-\:4-185] This included the creation of two CCP bodies for overseeing finance; the [Central Financial Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Financial_Commission) (CFC), as well as the revival of the [Central Financial Work Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Financial_Work_Commission) (CFWC) that was previously dissolved in 2002.[^cite_note-\:4-185] Additionally, a new CCP [Central Science and Technology Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Science_and_Technology_Commission) would be established to broadly oversee the technology sector, while a newly created [Society Work Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_Work_Department) was tasked with CCP interactions with several sectors, including civic groups, chambers of commerce and industry groups, as well as handling public petition and grievance work.[^cite_note-\:4-185] Regulatory bodies saw large overhauls.[^cite_note-186] Several regulatory responsibilities were also transferred from the [People's Bank of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Bank_of_China) (PBC) to another regulatory body, while the PBC reopened offices around the country that were closed in a previous reorganization.[^cite_note-187] In 2024, the CCP's role was strengthened further with the [Organic Law of the State Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_Law_of_the_State_Council) amended to add a clause about following CCP ideology and policies.[^cite_note-188]

#### Legal reforms

See also: [Xi Jinping Thought on the Rule of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought_on_the_Rule_of_Law)

> Efforts should be made to enable the people to see that justice is served in every judicial case.

— Xi Jinping during a speech in November 2020

[^cite_note-189]

The party under Xi announced a raft of [legal reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_reform) at the fourth plenum held in the fall 2014, and he called for "[Chinese socialistic rule of law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_of_law_in_China)" immediately afterwards. The party aimed to reform the legal system, which had been perceived as ineffective at delivering justice and affected by corruption, local government interference and lack of constitutional oversight. The plenum, while emphasizing the absolute leadership of the party, also called for a greater role of the constitution in the affairs of state and a strengthening of the role of the National People's Congress Standing Committee in interpreting the constitution.[^cite_note-190] It also called for more transparency in legal proceedings, more involvement of ordinary citizens in the legislative process, and an overall "professionalization" of the legal workforce. The party also planned to institute cross-jurisdictional circuit legal tribunals as well as giving provinces consolidated administrative oversight over lower level legal resources, which is intended to reduce local government involvement in legal proceedings.[^cite_note-191]

There have been several reforms to the court system under Xi, including transferring the authority over local court finance and personnel from local governments to the provincial-level, establishing a personnel quota system, emphasizing "lifetime accountability" for judges for their judicial decisions, and specification of case transferring procedures between various courts.[^cite_note-192] In 2014, China started efforts to pass a unified [Civil Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Code_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China), which was eventually adopted in 2020.[^cite_note-193] In 2015, Xi's administration oversaw the establishment of two circuit courts under the Supreme People's Court, followed by four other circuit courts being established in 2016.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudolf20213-194] [Hangzhou Internet Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hangzhou_Internet_Court) was established as a court of special jurisdiction in 2017, followed by the establishment of Beijing Internet Court and Guangzhou Internet Court.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2024206-195] [Shanghai Financial Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Financial_Court) was established in 2018 as a specialized financial court.[^cite_note-196] Under Xi, an increasing share of laws passed by the NPC explicitly affirmed the leadership of the CCP, with the share increasing from 4% in 2018 to nearly 70% in 2024.[^cite_note-197]

#### Military reforms

See also: [Modernization of the People's Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernization_of_the_People's_Liberation_Army), [Deepening National Defense and Military Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deepening_National_Defense_and_Military_Reform), and [Xi Jinping Thought on Strengthening the Military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought_on_Strengthening_the_Military)

> In the USSR, where the military was depoliticized, separated from the party and nationalized, the party was disarmed. When the Soviet Union came to crisis point, a big party was gone just like that. Proportionally, the Soviet Communist Party had more members than we do, but nobody was man enough to stand up and resist.

— Xi Jinping during a speech

[^cite_note-\:12aa-198]

Since taking power in 2012, Xi has undertaken an overhaul of the [People's Liberation Army](66890.md), including both political reform and its modernization.[^cite_note-Lague-2019-199] [Military-civil fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military-civil_fusion) has advanced under Xi.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBitzinger20215–24-200][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaniaLaskai20216-201] Xi has been active in his participation in military affairs, taking a direct hands-on approach to military reform. In addition to being the chairman of the CMC and leader of the [Central Leading Group for Military Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Leading_Group_for_Military_Reform) founded in 2014 to oversee comprehensive military reforms, Xi has delivered numerous high-profile pronouncements vowing to clean up malfeasance and complacency in the military. Xi has repeatedly warned that the depoliticization of the PLA from the CCP would lead to a collapse similar to that of the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-202] Xi held the New Gutian Conference in 2014, gathering China's top military officers, re-emphasizing the principle of "[the party has absolute control over the army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Party_commands_the_gun)" first established by Mao at the 1929 [Gutian Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gutian_Congress).[^cite_note-203] Xi's tenure saw an emphasis on the [chairman responsibility system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_responsibility_system), highlighting the CMC chairman's absolute leadership over the Central Military Commission and the military.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJi202631–32-204]



Xi and Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) reviewing [People's Liberation Army](66890.md) troops during a welcome ceremony in Beijing, 20 May 2026

At the [2015 China Victory Day Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_China_Victory_Day_Parade), Xi announced a reduction of 300,000 troops from the PLA, bringing its size to 2 million troops. Xi described this as a gesture of peace, while analysts have said that the cut was done to reduce costs as well as to modernize the PLA.[^cite_note-205] In November 2015, the Central Leading Group for Military Reform announced extensive reforms in the PLA called [Deepening National Defense and Military Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deepening_National_Defense_and_Military_Reform).[^cite_note-\:25-206] In 2016, Xi reduced the number of [theater commands of the PLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theater_command) from seven to five.[^cite_note-207] He also abolished the four autonomous general departments of the PLA, replacing them with 15 agencies directly reporting to the CMC.[^cite_note-Lague-2019-199] Two new branches of the PLA were created under his reforms, the [Strategic Support Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_Strategic_Support_Force)[^cite_note-208] and the [Joint Logistics Support Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_Joint_Logistics_Support_Force).[^cite_note-209] PLA Second Artillery Corps was upgraded to the [PLA Rocket Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_Rocket_Force).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllenAllen20175-210] In March 2016, the CMC announced the [abolishment of paid services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cessation_of_paid_services_by_the_Chinese_military) by the PLA and the [People's Armed Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Armed_Police) (PAP) by 2019, meaning activities like military-run hospitals and hotels open to the public.[^cite_note-211] On 21 April 2016, Xi was named [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Military_Command_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) of the country's new [CMC Joint Operations Command Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Military_Commission_Joint_Operations_Command_Center).[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213] In 2018, the PAP was placed under the sole control of the CMC; it was previously under the joint command of the CMC and the State Council through the Ministry of Public Security.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWuthnow201915-214] The PLA went through further extensive reforms in 2024; the Strategic Support Force was dissolved and the [Aerospace Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_Aerospace_Force), the [Cyberspace Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_Cyberspace_Force) and the [Information Support Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_Information_Support_Force) was established as arms of the PLA, joining the Joint Logistics Support Force.[^cite_note-\:24-215]

Under Xi, [China's official military budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_budget_of_China) has more than doubled,[^cite_note-\:8-216] reaching a record \$282 billion in 2026.[^cite_note-217] Though predating Xi, his administration has taken a more assertive stance towards maritime affairs, and has boosted CCP control over the maritime security forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiura2022287-218] The [PLA Navy](24918.md) has grown rapidly under Xi, with China adding more warships, submarines, support ships and major amphibious vessels from 2014 to 2019 than the entire number of ships serving in the United Kingdom's fleet.[^cite_note-219] Aircraft carriers *[Shandong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_aircraft_carrier_Shandong)* and *[Fujian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_aircraft_carrier_Fujian)* entered into service under Xi in 2019 and 2025 respectively.[^cite_note-220] In 2017, China established the navy's first [overseas base in Djibouti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Liberation_Army_Support_Base_in_Djibouti).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESunZoubir2021677–692-221] Xi has also undertaken an expansion of China's nuclear arsenal, with him calling China to "establish a strong system of strategic deterrence." The [Federation of American Scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_American_Scientists) (FAS) has estimated China's total nuclear arsenal to be 600 in 2025, with the [US Department of Defense](7279897.md) estimating that China's arsenal could reach 1,000 by 2030.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKristensenKordaJohnsKnight2025135–160-222]

### Economy and technology

Main article: [Economy of China](19284336.md)

See also: [Xi Jinping Thought on Economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought_on_Economy)

The *Decision* of the third plenary session of the 18th Central Committee announced "market forces" would begin to play a "decisive" role in allocating resources.[^cite_note-brook2-166][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhyte2021115–134-223] This meant that the state would gradually reduce its involvement in the distribution of capital, and restructure [state-owned enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned_enterprises_of_China) (SOEs) to allow further competition, potentially by attracting foreign and private sector players in industries previously highly regulated. This policy aimed to address the bloated state sector that had unduly profited from re-structuring by purchasing assets at below-market prices, assets no longer being used productively. However, by 2017, Xi's promise of economic reforms was said to have stalled by external observers.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhyte2021115–134-223] In 2015, the [Chinese stock market bubble popped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015%E2%80%932016_Chinese_stock_market_turbulence), which led Xi to use state forces to fix it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong2023146-225] China's economy has grown under Xi, more than doubling from CN¥54.8 trillion (US\$8.7 trillion) in 2012 to CN¥140.2 trillion (US\$20.1 trillion) in 2025, while China's nominal GDP per capita increased from CN¥40,431 (US\$6,408) in 2012 to CN¥99,665 (US\$14,318) in 2025,[^cite_note-226] surpassing the world average in 2021,[^cite_note-227] though growth has slowed from 7.9% in 2012 to 5% in 2025.[^cite_note-228]

Xi has increased state control over the economy, voicing support for SOEs,[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhyte2021115–134-223] while also supporting the private sector.[^cite_note-230] CCP control of SOEs has increased, while limited steps towards market liberalization, such as increasing mixed ownership of SOEs were undertaken.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiekeHofman202248-231] Under Xi, an increasing number of private companies have established Party branches.[^cite_note-232] From 2012 to 2022, the share of the market value of private sector firms in China's top listed companies increased from 10% to over 40%.[^cite_note-\:0-233] He has overseen the relaxation of restrictions on [foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) (FDI) and increased cross-border holdings of stocks and bonds.[^cite_note-\:0-233] His administration made it easier for banks to issue [mortgages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortgage_loan), increased foreign participation in the bond market, and increased the national currency [renminbi](66958.md)'s global role, helping it to join [IMF's](15251.md) basket of [special drawing right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_drawing_rights).[^cite_note-234] Xi launched the [Shanghai Free-Trade Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Free-Trade_Zone) in 2013.[^cite_note-235] In 2018, Xi announced the [Shanghai Stock Exchange STAR Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Stock_Exchange_STAR_Market), which opened in 2019.[^cite_note-guardian-236] He also led the establishment of the [Hainan Free Trade Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainan_Free_Trade_Port).[^cite_note-237] In 2020, *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported that Xi ordered a halt to [Ant Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant_Group)'s [initial public offering](63879.md) (IPO), in reaction to its founder [Jack Ma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Ma) criticizing government regulation in finance.[^cite_note-238] Under Xi, [government guidance funds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Government_Guidance_Fund), public-private investment funds set up by or for government bodies, have raised more than \$900 billion for early funding to companies that work in sectors the government deems as strategic.[^cite_note-Lockett-2022-239] Xi's administration has overseen a decrease in offshore IPOs by Chinese companies, with most Chinese IPOs taking place either in Shanghai or Shenzhen as of 2022, and has increasingly directed funding to IPOs of companies that works in sectors it deems as strategic, including [electric vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_vehicle_industry_in_China), [biotechnology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biotechnology_industry_in_China), [renewable energy](13556279.md), [artificial intelligence](57024219.md), [semiconductors](70425236.md) and other high-technology manufacturing.[^cite_note-Lockett-2022-239]

Xi has formulated the [new concept for development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_concept_for_development), stressing the importance of [high-quality development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-quality_development) rather than "inflated growth".[^cite_note-240] He has stated China has abandoned a growth-at-all-costs strategy which Xi refers to as "GDP heroism".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquisQiao202222-241] Instead, Xi said other social issues such as environmental protection are important.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquisQiao202222-241] Xi has made eradicating extreme poverty through [targeted poverty alleviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targeted_Poverty_Alleviation) a key goal.[^cite_note-242] In 2015, he launched the [battle against poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_against_poverty).[^cite_note-243] The campaign concluded by 2021, when Xi declared a "complete victory" over extreme poverty, saying nearly 100 million have been lifted out of poverty under his tenure, though some experts said China's [poverty threshold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_threshold) was lower than that of the [World Bank](36752.md).[^cite_note-244] In 2020, premier Li Keqiang, citing the [National Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bureau_of_Statistics_of_China) (NBS) said that China still had 600 million people living with less than 1000 yuan (\$140) a month, although *The Economist* said the methodology NBS used was flawed.[^cite_note-245] When Xi took office in 2012, 58% of people in China were living on less than \$8.30 per day, in 2022 this had fallen to 21%.[^cite_note-246] At the 19th Party Congress in 2017, Xi stated the [primary contradiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialectical_materialism) of China's conditions in the new era as "the contradiction between the people's ever-growing need for a better life and unbalanced and inadequate development."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELan2024267–268-247] In this context, "unbalanced" refers to rural-urban inequalities, regional inequalities, inequalities between the rich and poor, and structural imbalances in the economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELan2024268-248] "Inadequate" refers to household income share.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELan2024268-248]

Xi has overseen regional economic development initiatives within China such as the [Coordinated Development of the Beijing-Tianjin-Hebei Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordinated_Development_of_the_Beijing-Tianjin-Hebei_Region),[^cite_note-249] [Strategy for Integrated Development of the Yangtze River Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategy_for_Integrated_Development_of_the_Yangtze_River_Delta)[^cite_note-250] and the [Guangdong–Hong Kong–Macao Greater Bay Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangdong%E2%80%93Hong_Kong%E2%80%93Macao_Greater_Bay_Area).[^cite_note-\:7-251] Xi has been involved in the development of [Xiong'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiong'an), a [new area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_areas) announced in 2017, planned to become a major metropolis near Beijing; the relocation aspect is estimated to last until 2035 while it is planned to developed into a "modern socialist city" by 2050.[^cite_note-252] Additionally, Xi's administration has accelerated efforts to further integrate border regions such as [Tibet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibet_Autonomous_Region) and [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) with the rest of China.[^cite_note-253] Xi supports [dual circulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_circulation), a reorientation of the economy towards domestic consumption while remaining open to foreign trade and investment.[^cite_note-254] Xi has prioritised boosting [productivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Productivity).[^cite_note-255] Xi has attempted to reform the property sector to combat the steep increase in prices and cut the economy's dependence on it.[^cite_note-256] In the 19th CCP National Congress, Xi declared "[houses are for living, not for speculation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houses_are_for_living,_not_for_speculation)."[^cite_note-257] In 2020, Xi's government formulated the "[three red lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_red_lines)" policy that aimed to deleverage the heavily indebted property sector.[^cite_note-258] Xi has supported a [property tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_tax), for which he has faced resistance from members of the CCP.[^cite_note-259] His administration pursued a debt-deleveraging campaign, seeking to slow and cut the unsustainable amount of debt China has accrued during its growth.[^cite_note-260] Since 2021, China has faced a [property sector crisis](68779824.md), with decreasing house prices, shrinking of the real estate sector and bankruptcies of many property developers, partially as a result of Xi's efforts to decrease the sector's role in the Chinese economy.[^cite_note-261]

Xi has heavily emphasized the role of advanced manufacturing and technology development to drive China's future economic growth.[^cite_note-262] Since shortly after taking office as General Secretary, Xi emphasised the use of industrial policy to increase domestic innovation and reduce reliance on foreign technology.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorst2025105-263] In 2015, Xi's administration launched the [Double First-Class Construction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_First-Class_Construction), a higher education development and sponsorship scheme intended to replace the previous programs [Project 211](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_211) and [Project 985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_985).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELiXue20211–33-264] Xi's administration has promoted [Made in China 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Made_in_China_2025) plan that aims to make China self-reliant in key technologies; although publicly China de-emphasised this plan due to the outbreak of a [China–United States trade war](57055272.md), majority of its goals were considered achieved by 2024.[^cite_note-\:6-265] Since the outbreak of the trade war in 2018, Xi has further pushed calls for "self-reliance", especially on technology.[^cite_note-266] China's domestic spending on R\&D has significantly increased, surpassing the European Union (EU) and reaching a record \$564 billion in 2020.[^cite_note-\:8-216] The Chinese government has supported technology companies like [Huawei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huawei) through grants, tax breaks, credit facilities and other assistance, enabling their rise, leading to US countermeasures.[^cite_note-267] In 2023, Xi put forward [new productive forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_productive_forces), this refers to a new form of productive forces derived from continuous sci-tech breakthroughs and innovation that drive strategic emerging and future industries in a more intelligent [information era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_Age).[^cite_note-\:02-268] Under Xi, China made rapid advances in key technological areas, becoming a world leader in tech such as [electric vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plug-in_electric_vehicles_in_China), [lithium batteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_batteries_in_China) and solar panels,[^cite_note-\:6-265] as well as emerging as a global leader in [artificial intelligence](57024219.md).[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270]

> Common prosperity is an essential requirement of socialism and a key feature of Chinese-style modernization. The common prosperity we are pursuing is for all, affluence both in material and spiritual life, but not for a small portion nor for uniform egalitarianism.

— Xi Jinping during a speech in 2021

[^cite_note-Caixin_Global-2021-271]

[Income inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality_in_China) decreased during Xi's tenure.[^cite_note-272] Since 2021, Xi has promoted the term [common prosperity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_prosperity), which he defined as an "essential requirement of socialism", described as affluence for all and said entailed reasonable adjustments to excess incomes.[^cite_note-Caixin_Global-2021-271][^cite_note-273] Common prosperity has been used as the justification for [large-scale crackdowns and regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_Xi_Jinping_Administration_reform_spree) towards the perceived "excesses" of several sectors, most prominently tech and tutoring industries.[^cite_note-274] Actions taken include fining large tech companies[^cite_note-275] and passing laws such as the [Data Security Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_Security_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China). China introduced severe restrictions on private tutoring in the name of promoting social equality, effectively eliminating the private education industry.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorst2025131,_173–174-277] Xi opened a [new stock exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Stock_Exchange) in Beijing targeted for small and medium enterprises (SMEs).[^cite_note-278] There have been other cultural regulations including restrictions on minors playing video games and crackdowns on celebrity culture.[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] The push for common prosperity has also included salary and bonus cuts, especially across the financial sector,[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282] as well as crackdowns on wealth flaunting.[^cite_note-283]

### Foreign policy

See also: [Foreign policy of Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_Xi_Jinping), [Xi Jinping Thought on Diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought_on_Diplomacy), and [List of international trips made by Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_trips_made_by_Xi_Jinping)



Xi together with North Korean leader [Kim Jong Un](5962511.md), Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) and Pakistani Prime Minister [Shehbaz Sharif](6502561.md) during the [2025 China Victory Day Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_China_Victory_Day_Parade)
