Xi has taken a harder line on security issues as well as foreign affairs, projecting a more [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_nationalism) and assertive China on the world stage.[^cite_note-284] His political program calls for a China more united and confident of its own value system and political structure.[^cite_note-285] Foreign analysts and observers have frequently said that Xi's main foreign policy objective is to restore China's position on the global stage as a [great power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_power).[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-\:12aa-198][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrassInkster201841–68-287] Xi advocates "baseline thinking" in China's foreign policy: setting explicit red lines that other countries must not cross.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhao202386-288] In the Chinese perspective, these tough stances on baseline issues reduce strategic uncertainty, preventing other nations from misjudging China's positions or underestimating China's resolve in asserting what it perceives to be in its national interest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhao202386-288] Xi stated during the 20th CCP National Congress that he wanted to ensure China "leads the world in terms of [composite national strength](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_National_Power) and international influence" by 2049.[^cite_note-289]



World leaders assemble for "family photo" at [G20 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_G20_Hamburg_summit) in Hamburg.

Xi's foreign policy thoughts are collectively known as [Xi Jinping Thought on Diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought_on_Diplomacy).[^cite_note-290] Xi has promoted [major-country diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major-country_diplomacy), stating that China is already a "big power" and breaking away from previous Chinese leaders who had a more precautious diplomacy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHu20191–14-291] Diplomats under Xi have adopted a hawkish foreign policy posture called "[wolf warrior diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf_warrior_diplomacy)" starting from the late 2010s;[^cite_note-292] this form of diplomacy peaked in the early 2020s and declined afterwards.[^cite_note-\:9-293] Xi frequently says that the world is seeing "[great changes unseen in a century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_changes_unseen_in_a_century)", referring to geopolitical shifts in which the United States is seen as a declining power and in which the rise of populism, economic securitization, and advancing technology create an environment of uncertainty that results in both opportunities and threats for China.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBachulskaLeonardOertel2024233-294] In October 2020, he said that "[the East is rising and the West is declining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_East_is_rising_and_the_West_is_declining)", saying that the power of the Western world was in decline as exemplified by their COVID-19 response, and that China was entering a period of opportunity because of this.[^cite_note-295]

Xi has frequently alluded to [community with a shared future for mankind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_of_Common_Destiny), which Chinese diplomats have said does not imply an intention to change the international order,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhang2018196–207-296] but which foreign observers say China wants a new order that puts it more at the centre.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETobin2018157-297] During the Xi administration, China seeks to shape international norms and rules in emerging policy areas where China has an advantage as an early participant.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024188-298] Xi describes such areas as "new frontiers", and they include policy areas such as space, deep sea, polar regions, the Internet, nuclear safety, anticorruption, and climate change.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024188-298] Xi has put an emphasis on increasing China's "international discourse power" (国际话语权) to create a more favorable global opinion of China in the world.[^cite_note-299] In this pursuit, Xi has emphasised the need to "[tell China's story well](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telling_China's_story_well)", meaning expanding China's external propaganda (外宣) and communications.[^cite_note-300] Xi has expanded the focus and scope of the [united front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_front_\(China\)), which aims to consolidate support for CCP in non-CCP elements both inside and outside China, and has accordingly expanded the United Front Work Department.[^cite_note-301]



Xi and Japanese Prime Minister [Shinzo Abe](2634434.md) in Beijing, December 2019

In 2013, Xi announced the [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative) (BRI), a global infrastructure and economic development project.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarlick202475-302] Encompassing much of Africa and Eurasia, the BRI became central to Chinese foreign policy and is the largest infrastructure investment by a great power since the Marshall Plan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtisKlaus20241-303] In 2015, Xi announced the founding of the [Asia Infrastructure Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Infrastructure_Investment_Bank), seen as a competitor to other multilateral banks such as the [World Bank](36752.md) and the [Asia Development Bank](2512.md).[^cite_note-304] Xi has unveiled the [Global Development Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Development_Initiative) (GDI),[^cite_note-305] the [Global Security Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Security_Initiative) (GSI),[^cite_note-306] the [Global Civilisation Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Civilization_Initiative) (GCI), and the [Global Governance Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Governance_Initiative) (GGI)[^cite_note-307] in 2021, 2022, 2023 and 2025 respectively, aiming to increase China's influence in the international order.[^cite_note-\:12-308] Under Xi, China has, along with Russia, also focused on increasing relations with the [Global South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_North_and_Global_South) in order to blunt the effect of [international sanctions](70323386.md).[^cite_note-309]

#### Africa

&#x20;During Xi's administration, China has maintained cordial relationships with each Africa government except [Eswatini](27451.md), which recognizes Taiwan but not the PRC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShinnEisenman20233-310] Under Xi, China has cut back lending to Africa after fears that African countries could not repay their debts to China.[^cite_note-311] Xi has also promised that China would write off debts of some African countries.[^cite_note-312] In 2025, China announced that it would remove nearly all import tariffs on African countries.[^cite_note-313]



Xi meeting Indian Prime Minister [Narendra Modi](444222.md) at the [16th BRICS Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_BRICS_Summit) in [Kazan](202295.md), Russia, October 2024

#### Asia

Under Xi, China initially took a more critical stance on North Korea due to [its nuclear tests](106424.md).[^cite_note-chengli232-314] However, starting in 2018, the relations started to improve due to [meetings between Xi and North Korean leader Kim Jong Un](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim%E2%80%93Xi_meetings).[^cite_note-315] Xi has initially improved relationships with South Korea,[^cite_note-chengli232-314] and the two countries signed a [free-trade agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93South_Korea_Free_Trade_Agreement) in December 2015.[^cite_note-\:22-316] Starting in 2017, China's relationship with South Korea soured over the [Terminal High Altitude Area Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminal_High_Altitude_Area_Defense) (THAAD), a missile defense system, [deployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/THAAD_deployment_by_South_Korea) of the latter, but improved after South Korea halted further deployments on the THAAD.[^cite_note-317] China–Japan relations have initially soured under Xi's administration; the most thorny issue between the two countries remains the dispute over the [Senkaku Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senkaku_Islands), which China calls Diaoyu.[^cite_note-osawa22-318] However, the relations later started to improve,[^cite_note-319] though [deteriorated in 2025](81642186.md) after comments made by Prime Minister [Sanae Takaichi](8125532.md) regarding a potential defense of [Taiwan](25734.md).[^cite_note-320]

Since Xi came to power, China has been rapidly building and militarizing islands in the South China Sea, a decision *[Study Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Study_Times)* of the [Central Party School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Party_School) said was personally taken by Xi.[^cite_note-321] Relations between China and India had ups and downs under Xi. The two countries had a [standoff in Depsang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depsang_standoff) in 2013,[^cite_note-322] and [again had a standoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_China%E2%80%93India_border_standoff) over a Chinese construction of a road in [Doklam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doklam), a territory both claimed by [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan), India's ally, and China, in 2017.[^cite_note-323] The most serious crisis in the relationship came when the two countries had a [deadly clash in 2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932021_China%E2%80%93India_skirmishes) at the [Line of Actual Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Actual_Control), leaving some soldiers dead.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] The relationship later improved starting from 2024.[^cite_note-326] While China has historically been wary of getting closer to the [Middle East](19323.md) countries, Xi has changed this approach, getting closer to both Iran and Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-Vohra-2022-327]

#### Europe



Xi with French President [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) and European Commission President [Ursula von der Leyen](2991932.md) during his [state visit to France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_visits_by_Xi_Jinping_to_France,_Serbia_and_Hungary), 6 May 2024

China's efforts under Xi has been for the European Union (EU) to stay in a neutral position in their contest with the U.S.[^cite_note-328] China and the EU announced the [Comprehensive Agreement on Investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Agreement_on_Investment) (CAI) in 2020, although the deal was later frozen due to mutual sanctions over Xinjiang.[^cite_note-329]

Xi has cultivated [stronger relations with Russia](30964882.md), particularly in the wake of the [Ukraine crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_pro-Russian_unrest_in_Ukraine) of 2014.[^cite_note-russia222-330] Following the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) and during the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md), Xi expressed opposition to [sanctions against Russia](43449324.md)[^cite_note-331] and asserted China's [support for Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_and_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) on issues of sovereignty and security, but also said China is committed to respecting "the territorial integrity of all countries",[^cite_note-332] while China has cast itself as a neutral party.[^cite_note-Xi-Putin-333] Xi's stance during the war has been criticized by [Ukraine](31750.md) and several Western governments,[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] which argued that China's support for Russia and its defense industrial base was inconsistent with its stated neutrality.[^cite_note-336]

#### United States



With U.S. president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in Beijing during his [visit to China](83136377.md), May 2026

Xi has called [China–United States relations](277880.md) in the contemporary world a "new type of great-power relations", a phrase the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama) had been reluctant to embrace.[^cite_note-nikkei2-337] Xi has indirectly spoken out critically on the U.S. "strategic pivot" to Asia.[^cite_note-blan2-338] Relations with the U.S. soured after [Donald Trump](4848272.md) became president in 2017.[^cite_note-339] Since 2018, U.S. and China have been engaged in an escalating [trade war](57055272.md).[^cite_note-340] In 2020, the relations further deteriorated due to the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-341]

### National security

Xi has devoted a large amount of work towards national security, calling for the establishment of a [holistic national security concept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holistic_national_security) that encompasses "all aspects of the work of the party and the country."[^cite_note-342] He introduced the concept in 2014, which he defined as taking "the security of the people as compass, political security as its roots, economic security as its pillar, military security and cultural security as its protections, and that relies on the promotion of international security."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShinnEisenman20233-310] A new [National Security Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Commission_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) was formed in 2014 with Xi at its helm, centralizing national security decision-making.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELampton2015759–777-344] Since its creation by Xi, the National Security Commission has established local security committees, focusing on dissent.[^cite_note-Buckley-2022-345]

Believing it to be an important foundation of national security, Xi's administration has prioritized efforts to establish [food security](216361.md) by pushing for as much as self-sufficiency in food as possible, while launching the [Clean Plate campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_Plate_campaign) in 2013 and 2020 to combat food waste.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFangLi202438-346][^cite_note-guardian-20201223-347] He has additionally given priority to energy security; China is the world's largest energy consumer, having consumed more energy than the United States, the European Union and Japan combined in 2024. China has made heavy investments in its energy grid and designed policies to lessen dependence on foreign energy imports by investing in renewable energy as well as [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_in_China).[^cite_note-348]

Xi has championed the [Fengqiao experience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fengqiao_experience), calling on officials to contain and resolve conflicts at the grassroots level without needing to involve higher legal bodies.[^cite_note-\:14-349] In the name of national security, Xi's government has passed numerous laws including the [Counterespionage Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterespionage_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) in 2014,[^cite_note-350] the [National Security Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China)[^cite_note-351] and the [Counterterrorism Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterterrorism_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) in 2015,[^cite_note-352] the [Cybersecurity Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cybersecurity_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China)[^cite_note-353] and the [Law on Administration of Foreign NGOs' Activities within China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_on_Administration_of_Foreign_NGOs'_Activities_within_China) in 2016,[^cite_note-354] the [National Intelligence Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) in 2017,[^cite_note-355] and the [Data Security Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_Security_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) in 2021.[^cite_note-356] Under Xi, [China's mass surveillance network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_surveillance_in_China) has dramatically grown, with comprehensive profiles being built for each citizen.[^cite_note-357] [Grid-style social management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grid-style_social_management_in_China), which involves subdividing China's counties into smaller zones, and assigning each zone to a person that reports all activity to the local government on a regular basis, has risen in prominence under Xi.[^cite_note-358] Public security spending has increased significantly under Xi, reaching \$210 billion in 2020, more than double from the decade prior; this was accompanied by a significant decrease in crime.[^cite_note-359] Xi's leadership has also seen a more prominent role taken by the [Ministry of State Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_State_Security_\(China\)), which expanded its public profile.[^cite_note-360]

### Hong Kong

See also: [Conservatism in Hong Kong § Xi Jinping era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_Hong_Kong#Xi_Jinping_era)



[Some Hong Kong protesters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_Hong_Kong_protests) throw eggs at Xi's portrait on [National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China).

During his leadership, Xi has supported and pursued a greater political and economic integration of [Hong Kong](13404.md) to mainland China, including through projects such as the [Hong Kong–Zhuhai–Macau Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong%E2%80%93Zhuhai%E2%80%93Macau_Bridge).[^cite_note-Bland-2018-361] He has pushed for the [Greater Bay Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangdong%E2%80%93Hong_Kong%E2%80%93Macao_Greater_Bay_Area) project, which aims to integrate Hong Kong, [Macau](19068.md), and nine other cities in Guangdong.[^cite_note-\:7-251][^cite_note-Bland-2018-361] Xi's integration efforts have led to deeper economic links between mainland China and Hong Kong, as well as decreased freedoms and the weakening of Hong Kong's distinct identity from mainland China.[^cite_note-\:7-251][^cite_note-362] Many of the views held by the central government and eventually implemented in Hong Kong were outlined in a white paper published by the [State Council Information Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Council_Information_Office) in 2014 named *[The Practice of the 'One Country, Two Systems' Policy in the Hong Kong Special Administrative Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Practice_of_the_'One_Country,_Two_Systems'_Policy_in_the_Hong_Kong_Special_Administrative_Region)*, which outlined that the scope of Hong Kong's autonomy is not inherent, but solely determined by the Central Authorities' delegation of power.[^cite_note-Buckley-2021b-363] Under Xi, the Chinese government also declared the [Sino-British Joint Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-British_Joint_Declaration) to be legally void.[^cite_note-Buckley-2021b-363] In August 2014, the Standing Committee of the National People's Congress (NPCSC) made a [decision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_NPCSC_Decision_on_Hong_Kong) allowing universal suffrage for the [2017 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Hong_Kong_Chief_Executive_election) of the [chief executive of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Executive_of_Hong_Kong), also requiring the candidates to "love the country, and love Hong Kong," as well as other measures that ensured the Chinese leadership would be the ultimate decision-maker on the selection, leading to [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Hong_Kong_protests),[^cite_note-364] and the eventual rejection of the reform bill in the Legislative Council due to a walk-out by the [pro-Beijing camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-Beijing_camp_\(Hong_Kong\)) to delay to vote.[^cite_note-365]



Xi swearing in [John Lee Ka-chiu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lee_Ka-chiu) as chief executive during the 25th anniversary of Hong Kong's return to China

In the 2017 chief executive election, [Carrie Lam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrie_Lam) was victorious, reportedly with the endorsement of the CCP Politburo.[^cite_note-366] Xi supported the [Hong Kong Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Hong_Kong) and Carrie Lam against the protesters in the [2019–2020 Hong Kong protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_Hong_Kong_protests), which broke out after a [proposed bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Hong_Kong_extradition_bill) that would allow extraditions to mainland China.[^cite_note-367] He defended the [Hong Kong Police Force](1276176.md)'s use of force, saying that "We sternly support the Hong Kong police to take forceful actions in enforcing the law, and the Hong Kong judiciary to punish in accordance with the law those who have committed violent crimes."[^cite_note-368] While [visiting Macau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_visit_by_Xi_Jinping_to_Macau) on 20 December 2019 as part of the 20th anniversary of its return to China, Xi warned of foreign forces interfering in Hong Kong and Macau,[^cite_note-369] while also hinting that Macau could be a model for Hong Kong to follow.[^cite_note-370] In 2020, the NPCSC passed a [national security law](64033197.md) in Hong Kong that dramatically expanded government clampdown over the opposition in the city; amongst the measures were the dramatic restriction on political opposition and the creation of the [Office for Safeguarding National Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_for_Safeguarding_National_Security) outside Hong Kong jurisdiction to oversee the enforcement of the law.[^cite_note-Buckley-2021b-363] Xi visited Hong Kong as Chinese leader in [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_visit_by_Xi_Jinping_to_Hong_Kong) and [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_visit_by_Xi_Jinping_to_Hong_Kong), in the 20th and 25th anniversary of the handover of Hong Kong respectively.[^cite_note-371] During his 2017 visit to Hong Kong, Xi swore in Lam as chief executive.[^cite_note-372] In his 2022 visit, he swore in [John Lee Ka-chiu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lee_Ka-chiu) as chief executive, a former police officer that was backed by the Chinese government to expand control over the city.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374] Under Lee, Hong Kong adopted the [Safeguarding National Security Ordinance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safeguarding_National_Security_Ordinance) in 2024 to implement [Article 23](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_23) of the [Hong Kong Basic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Basic_Law), further criminalizing acts endangering national security.[^cite_note-nyt-375]

### Taiwan

Further information: [Cross-Strait relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-Strait_relations) and [2015 meeting of Ma Ying-jeou and Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_meeting_of_Ma_Ying-jeou_and_Xi_Jinping)



Xi meeting with then-Taiwanese president [Ma Ying-jeou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Ying-jeou) in November 2015

In 2013, Xi coined the slogan "[both sides of the Taiwan Strait are one family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Both_sides_of_the_Taiwan_Strait_are_one_family)" regarding [mainland China](55079.md) and [Taiwan](25734.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGong202256-376] [Wang Yu-chi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Yu-chi), Minister of Taiwan's [Mainland Affairs Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Affairs_Council), spoke with [Zhang Zhijun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Zhijun), Director of the [Taiwan Affairs Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Affairs_Office), [met](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Wang%E2%80%93Zhang_meetings) in 2014, making the first official, high-level, government-to-government contact between the two sides since 1949.[^cite_note-377] In November 2015, Xi met with Taiwanese president [Ma Ying-jeou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Ying-jeou), which marked the first time the political leaders of both sides of the [Taiwan Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Strait) have met since the end of the [Chinese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Civil_War) in mainland China in 1950.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379] However, the relations started deteriorating after [Tsai Ing-wen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsai_Ing-wen) of the [Democratic Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party) (DPP) won the [2016 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Taiwanese_presidential_election).[^cite_note-380]

In the 19th Party Congress held in 2017, Xi reaffirmed six of the nine principles that had been affirmed continuously since the 16th Party Congress in 2002, with the notable exception of "Placing hopes on the [Taiwan people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_of_Taiwan) as a force to help bring about [unification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_unification)".[^cite_note-Brookings_R.C._Bush2-381] According to the [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution), Xi used stronger language on potential [Taiwan independence](31156.md) than his predecessors towards previous DPP governments in Taiwan. Xi said that "we will never allow any person, any organisation, or any political party to split any part of the Chinese territory from China at any time at any form."[^cite_note-Brookings_R.C._Bush2-381] At the same time, he offered the chance for open talks and "unobstructed exchanges" with Taiwan as long as the government moved to accept the [1992 Consensus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Consensus).[^cite_note-\:06-382] Xi stated that people from Taiwan could receive national treatment in pursuing careers on the mainland and in February 2018 the PRC government announced 31 preferential policies for Taiwan people on matters of industry, finance, taxation, land use, employment, education, and health care.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhao202413-383]

In January 2019, Xi gave a speech commemorating the 40th anniversary of the *[Message to Compatriots in Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Message_to_Compatriots_in_Taiwan)*, outlining [five points](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi's_Five_Points) proposing unification under the ["one country, two systems" formula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_country,_two_systems_\(Taiwan_plan\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFangLi2024240-384] He called on Taiwan to reject formal independence from China, saying: "We make [no promise to renounce the use of force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Never_promise_to_renounce_the_use_of_force) and reserve the option of taking all necessary means." Those options, he said, could be used against "external interference". Xi also said that they "are willing to create broad space for peaceful reunification, but will leave no room for any form of separatist activities."[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386] President Tsai responded to the speech by saying Taiwan would not accept a one country, two systems arrangement with the mainland, while stressing the need for all cross-strait negotiations to be on a government-to-government basis.[^cite_note-387] Since 2018, the People's Liberation Army has conducted military drills around Taiwan with increasing frequency to pressure the DPP-led government.[^cite_note-388] In 2022, the Chinese government released *[The Taiwan Question and China's Reunification in the New Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Taiwan_Question_and_China's_Reunification_in_the_New_Era)*, outlining the PRC's official line on Taiwan under Xi.[^cite_note-\:17-389] In 2025, China established the "[Commemoration Day of Taiwan's Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retrocession_Day)", a decision which Taiwan Affairs Office director [Song Tao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_Tao_\(diplomat\)) said was done "personally" by Xi.[^cite_note-\:07-390][^cite_note-391]

### Human rights

Main articles: [Human rights in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_China) and [Antireligious campaigns in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antireligious_campaigns_in_China)

According to the [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), Xi has "started a broad and sustained offensive on human rights" since he became leader in 2012.[^cite_note-392] The HRW also said that repression in China is "at its worst level since the Tiananmen Square massacre."[^cite_note-393] Since taking power, Xi has cracked down on grassroots activism, with hundreds being detained.[^cite_note-394] He presided over the [709 crackdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/709_crackdown) on 9 July 2015, which saw more than 200 lawyers, legal assistants and human rights activists being detained.[^cite_note-395] His term has seen the arrest and imprisonment of activists such as [Xu Zhiyong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xu_Zhiyong), as well as numerous others who identified with the [New Citizens' Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Citizens'_Movement). Prominent legal activist [Pu Zhiqiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pu_Zhiqiang) of the [Weiquan movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weiquan_movement) was also arrested and detained.[^cite_note-396] According to activists, "Xi is waging the most severe systematic [suppression of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_China#Restrictions_and_international_interest) in the country since religious freedom was written into the Chinese constitution in 1982," and according to pastors and a group that monitors religion in China, has involved "destroying crosses, burning bibles, shutting churches and ordering followers to sign papers renouncing their faith."[^cite_note-397]

Under Xi, the CCP has embraced assimilationist policies towards ethnic minorities, scaling back [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action_in_China) in the country by 2019,[^cite_note-398] and scrapping a wording in October 2021 that guaranteed the rights of minority children to be educated in their native language, replacing it with one that emphasized teaching the national language.[^cite_note-399] In 2014, Xi called to foster a [sense of community for the Chinese nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sense_of_community_for_the_Chinese_nation) among ethnic minorities.[^cite_note-400] In 2020, [Chen Xiaojiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Xiaojiang) was appointed as head of the [National Ethnic Affairs Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Ethnic_Affairs_Commission), the first Han Chinese head of the body since 1954.[^cite_note-401] On 24 June 2022, [Pan Yue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_Yue_\(politician\)), another Han Chinese, became the head of the commission, with him reportedly holding assimilationist policies toward ethnic minorities.[^cite_note-402] Xi outlined his official views relations between the majority Han Chinese and ethnic minorities by saying "\[n]either [Han chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_chauvinism) nor [local ethnic chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_ethnic_chauvinism) is conducive to the development of a community for the [Chinese nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhonghua_minzu)."[^cite_note-403] Xi's policies toward ethnic minorities were codified in the [Law on Promoting Ethnic Unity and Progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_on_Promoting_Ethnic_Unity_and_Progress) in 2026.[^cite_note-\:15-404]

#### Xinjiang

Main article: [Persecution of Uyghurs in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Uyghurs_in_China)



Xi's picture at the entrance hall of the [Xinjiang Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_Museum) in [Ürümqi](199155.md) in August 2018

There were several terrorist attacks in [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang) in 2013 and 2014; an [attack in Ürümqi in April 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2014_%C3%9Cr%C3%BCmqi_attack) occurred just after the conclusion of a visit by Xi to Xinjiang.[^cite_note-405] Following these attacks, CCP leaders held a secret meeting to find a solution,[^cite_note-\:5-406] leading to Xi to launch the [Strike Hard Campaign Against Violent Terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike_Hard_Campaign_Against_Violent_Terrorism) in 2014, which involved mass detention, and surveillance of ethnic [Uyghurs](31783.md) there.[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-Ramzy-2019-408] The campaign included the detainment of 1.8 million people in [internment camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_internment_camps), mostly Uyghurs but also including other ethnic and religious minorities, by 2020,[^cite_note-\:5-406] and a birth suppression campaign that led to a large drop in the Uyghur birth rate by 2019.[^cite_note-409] Human rights groups and former inmates have said Uyghurs and other minorities have been forcibly assimilated into China's majority [ethnic Han](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese) society in the camps.[^cite_note-410] This program has been called a [genocide](12441.md) by some observers, while [a report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Human_Rights_Office_report_on_Xinjiang) by the [UN Human Rights Office](22463.md) said they may amount to [crimes against humanity](61099.md).[^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412]

[Internal Chinese government documents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang_papers) leaked to the press in November 2019 showed that Xi personally ordered a security crackdown in Xinjiang, saying that the party must show "absolutely no mercy" and that officials use all the "weapons of the [people's democratic dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_democratic_dictatorship)" to suppress those "infected with the virus of extremism."[^cite_note-Ramzy-2019-408][^cite_note-413] The papers also showed that Xi repeatedly discussed [Islamic extremism](8734632.md) in his speeches, likening it to a "virus" or a "drug" that could be only addressed by "a period of painful, interventionary treatment."[^cite_note-Ramzy-2019-408] However, he also warned against the discrimination against Uyghurs and rejected proposals to eradicate [Islam in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_China), calling that kind of viewpoint "biased, even wrong."[^cite_note-Ramzy-2019-408] Xi's exact role in the building of internment camps has not been publicly reported, though he's widely believed to be behind them and his words have been the source for major justifications in the crackdown in Xinjiang.[^cite_note-Economist-2022a-414][^cite_note-415] China began to wind down the camps in 2019, and some detainees were transferred to the [penal system](14298944.md), while others were transferred to [forced labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labor) and factory work programs.[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417] In a July 2022 visit to Xinjiang, Xi urged local officials to always listen to the citizens' voices[^cite_note-418] and to improve efforts in preserving ethnic minority culture.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420] In a visit to Xinjiang the following year, Xi stated that the region was "no longer a remote area" and should open up more for [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Xinjiang) to attract domestic and foreign visitors.[^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422]

### Education

See also: [Education in China](407843.md)

In 2014, the [Party General Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Office_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) and [State Council](44498.md) issued guidance on strengthening ideological education in colleges and universities.[^cite_note-423] During Xi Jinping's tenure, numerous colleges and universities have established schools of Marxism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi2024b332-424] Xi has implemented a number of education reforms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024155-425] Schools are required to adjust their opening hours to be consistent with work hours in their area so that parents can pick-up their children directly after work (in order to reduce reliance on private classes for adult supervision after school hours).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024155-425] Schools must also promote health by requiring outdoor physical education classes daily and providing eye examinations twice per term.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024155-425] Educational reforms have also limited the amount of homework students can be assigned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024156-426]

In 2021, Xi enacted the [Double Reduction Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_Reduction_Policy) (reducing excessive off-campus tutoring and reducing homework burdens), mandating schools may not assign homework to children to grades one and two, homework is limited to no more than 60 minutes for children in grades three to six, and no more than 90 minutes for middle school children.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024156-426] In July 2021, China enacted a series of rules designed to shutdown the private tutoring sector as part of its common prosperity program.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024156-426] Rules issued in July 2021 prohibits new registration of private tuition tutoring centers and required existing centers to re-organize as non-profits.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024156-426] Tuition centers are prohibited from being listed on the stock market or receiving "excessive capital."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024156-426] They are no longer permitted to offer tutoring on the weekends or during public holidays.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024156-426] Since September 2021, private schools providing compulsory education can no longer be controlled by foreign entities or individuals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung202457-137] Only Chinese nationals may serve on their boards of directors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung2024157-427] In 2023, the [Patriotic Education Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic_Education_Law_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) was adopted, codifying the mandatory [patriotic education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic_education_in_China) program in the country.[^cite_note-428]

### COVID-19 pandemic

See also: [Chinese government response to COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_government_response_to_COVID-19) and [COVID-19 pandemic in mainland China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_mainland_China)

On 20 January 2020, Xi commented for the first time on the emerging [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) in Wuhan, and ordered "efforts to curb the spread" of the virus.[^cite_note-429] He gave premier Li Keqiang some responsibility over the COVID-19 response, in what has been suggested by *The Wall Street Journal* was an attempt to potentially insulate himself from criticism if the response failed.[^cite_note-430] The government initially responded to the pandemic with a lockdown and censorship, with the initial response causing widespread backlash within China.[^cite_note-431] Xi met with [Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus](39712205.md), the director-general of the World Health Organization (WHO), on 28 January.[^cite_note-Steger-2020-432] *[Der Spiegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der_Spiegel)* reported that in January 2020 Xi pressured Tedros Adhanom to hold off on issuing a [global warning](42694003.md) about the outbreak of COVID-19 and hold back information on human-to-human transmission of the virus, allegations denied by the WHO.[^cite_note-433] On 5 February, Xi met with Cambodian prime minister [Hun Sen](393779.md) in Beijing, the first foreign leader allowed into China since the outbreak.[^cite_note-Steger-2020-432] After the COVID-19 outbreak got under control in Wuhan, Xi visited the city on 10 March.[^cite_note-434]

After getting the outbreak in Wuhan under control, Xi has favoured what has officially been termed "dynamic [zero-COVID policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero-COVID)"[^cite_note-435] that aims to control and suppress the virus as much as possible within the country's borders. This has involved local lockdowns and mass-testing.[^cite_note-Wong-2022-436] While initially credited for China's suppression of the COVID-19 outbreak, the policy was later criticized by foreign and some domestic observers for being out of touch with the rest of the world and taking a heavy toll on the economy.[^cite_note-Wong-2022-436] This approach has especially come under criticism during a 2022 lockdown on Shanghai, which forced millions to their homes and damaged the city's economy.[^cite_note-437] Conversely, Xi has said that the policy was designed to protect people's life safety.[^cite_note-438] On 23 July 2022, the [National Health Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Health_Commission) reported that Xi and other top leaders have taken the local COVID-19 vaccines.[^cite_note-439]

At the 20th CCP Congress, Xi confirmed the continuation of the zero-COVID policy,[^cite_note-440] stating he would "unswervingly" carry out "dynamic zero-COVID" and promising to "resolutely win the battle",[^cite_note-441] though China started a limited easing of the policies in the following weeks.[^cite_note-442] In November 2022, [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_COVID-19_protests_in_China) broke out against China's COVID-19 policies, with a [fire in a high-rise apartment building in Ürümqi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_%C3%9Cr%C3%BCmqi_fire) being the trigger.[^cite_note-Che-2022-443] The protests were held in multiple major cities, with some of the protesters demanding the end of Xi's and the CCP's rule.[^cite_note-Che-2022-443] The protests were mostly suppressed by December.[^cite_note-Che-2022-443] Follwing the protests, the government further eased COVID-19 restrictions.[^cite_note-444] On 7 December 2022, China announced large-scale changes to its COVID-19 policy, including allowing quarantine at home for mild infections, reducing of [PCR testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polymerase_chain_reaction), and decreasing the power of local officials to implement lockdowns, effectively ending the zero-COVID policy.[^cite_note-445]

### Environmental policy

See also: [Xi Jinping Thought on Ecological Civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought_on_Ecological_Civilization) and [Environmental policy in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_policy_in_China)

Xi identifies environmental protection as one of China's five major priorities for national progress.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi2024a164-446] Xi has popularized a metaphor of "two mountains" to emphasize the importance of environmental protection.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi2024a164-446] The concept is that a mountain made of gold or silver is valuable, but [green mountains with clear waters are more valuable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clear_waters_and_green_mountains).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi2024a164-446] The slogan's meaning is that economic development priorities must also provide for economic protection.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi2024a164-446]

In September 2020, Xi announced that China would "strengthen its 2030 climate target (NDC), peak emissions before 2030 and aim to achieve [carbon neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_neutrality) before 2060."[^cite_note-CAT-2020-447] If accomplished, this would lower the expected rise in global temperature by 0.2–0.3 °C – "the biggest single reduction ever estimated by the Climate Action Tracker."[^cite_note-CAT-2020-447] Xi mentioned the link between the COVID-19 pandemic and nature destruction as one of the reasons for the decision, saying that "Humankind can no longer afford to ignore the repeated warnings of nature."[^cite_note-448] On 27 September 2020, Chinese scientists presented a detailed plan how to achieve the target.[^cite_note-449] In September 2021, Xi announced that China will not build "coal-fired power projects abroad", which was said to be potentially "pivotal" in reducing emissions. The Belt and Road Initiative did not include financing such projects in the first half of 2021.[^cite_note-450] Xi did not attend [COP26](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference) personally. However, a Chinese delegation led by climate change envoy [Xie Zhenhua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xie_Zhenhua_\(politician\)) did attend.[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452] During the conference, the United States and China agreed on a framework to reduce [GHG emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GHG_emission) by co-operating on different measures.[^cite_note-453]

### Governance style

Known as a very secretive leader, little is known publicly about how Xi makes political decisions, or how he came to power.[^cite_note-Economist-2022b-454][^cite_note-Myers-2018-455] Xi's speeches generally get released months or years after they are made.[^cite_note-Economist-2022b-454] Xi has also never given a press conference since becoming paramount leader, except in rare joint press conferences with foreign leaders.[^cite_note-Economist-2022b-454][^cite_note-456] *The Wall Street Journal* reported that Xi prefers micromanaging in governance, in contrast to previous leaders such as [Hu Jintao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Jintao) who left details of major policies to lower-ranking officials.[^cite_note-Chin-2021-135] Reportedly, ministerial officials try to get Xi's attention in various ways, with some creating slide shows and audio reports. *The Wall Street Journal* also reported that Xi created a performance-review system in 2018 to give evaluations on officials on various measures, including loyalty.[^cite_note-Chin-2021-135] According to *The Economist*, Xi's orders have generally been vague, leaving lower level officials to interpret his words.[^cite_note-Economist-2022a-414]

Chinese state media Xinhua News Agency said that Xi "personally reviews every draft of major policy documents" and "all reports submitted to him, no matter how late in the evening, were returned with instructions the following morning."[^cite_note-Blachard-2017-457] With regard to behavior of Communist Party members, Xi emphasizes the "Two Musts" (members must not be arrogant or rash and must keep their hard-working spirit) and the "Six Nos" (members must say no to formalism, bureaucracy, gift-giving, luxurious birthday celebrations, hedonism, and extravagance).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquisQiao202252-458] Xi called for officials to practice [self-criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_and_self-criticism) which, according to observers, is in order to appear less corrupt and more popular among the people.[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460][^cite_note-461]

## Political positions

Further information: [Views of Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Views_of_Xi_Jinping)

### Chinese Dream

Main article: [Chinese Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Dream)



According to *[Qiushi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qiushi)*, the Chinese Dream is about Chinese prosperity, collective effort, socialism, and national glory.

Xi and CCP ideologues coined the phrase "Chinese Dream" to describe his overarching plans for China as its leader. Xi first used the phrase during a high-profile visit to the [National Museum of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_China) on 29 November 2012, where he and his Politburo Standing Committee colleagues were attending a "national revival" exhibition. Since then, the phrase has become the signature political slogan of the Xi era.[^cite_note-462][^cite_note-463] The origin of the term "Chinese Dream" is unclear. While the phrase has been used before by journalists and scholars,[^cite_note-464] some publications have posited the term likely drew its inspiration from the concept of the [American Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Dream).[^cite_note-465] *The Economist* noted the abstract and seemingly accessible nature of the concept with no specific overarching policy stipulations may be a deliberate departure from the jargon-heavy ideologies of his predecessors.[^cite_note-466] Xi has linked the "Chinese Dream" with the phrase "[great rejuvenation of the Chinese nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_rejuvenation_of_the_Chinese_nation)".[^cite_note-467]

### Culture

See also: [Xi Jinping Thought on Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought_on_Culture), [Cultural policy of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_policy_of_China), and [Religious policy of Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_policy_of_Xi_Jinping)

Xi has called traditional Chinese culture the "soul" of the nation and the "foundation" of the CCP's culture.[^cite_note-\:1-468] He has praised the "splendid Chinese civilization", calling the CCP's governance part of "the uninterrupted development of a civilization for several thousand years" which is "rarely seen among nations in the world".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung202487-469] Xi has also called for integrating the basic tenets of Marxism with China's traditional culture.[^cite_note-\:12-308] He has established the "[Four Confidences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Confidences)", which has later been added to the CCP constitution, calling for CCP members, government officials and the Chinese people to be "confident in our chosen path, confident in our guiding theories, confident in our political system, and confident in our culture." He unveiled Global Civilization Initiative in 2023, calling for "respecting the diversity of civilizations, advocating the common values of humanity, valuing the inheritance and innovation of civilizations, and strengthening international people-to-people exchanges and cooperation".[^cite_note-\:12-308]

> &#x20;A party and its authority rests on winning the hearts and minds of the people. What the public opposes and hates, we must address and resolve.
>
> &#x20;\[M]aterial and cultural needs grown; demands for democracy, rule of law, fairness and justice, security, and a better environment are also increasing each day.

— Xi Jinping during a speech in 2017

[^cite_note-voa2017oct-470]

Xi supports a socialist artistic revival, including the promotion of patriotic art and red classics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETu2025253-471] Since the [18th Party Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), Xi has emphasized utilizing red resources, telling red stories, and inheriting red genes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi202412-472] On 15 October 2014, Xi emulated the [Yan'an Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yan'an_Forum) with his '[Speech at the Forum on Literature and Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Forum_on_Literature_and_Art).'[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESorace201915-473] Consistent with Mao's view in the Yan'an Talks, Xi believes works of art should be judged by political criteria.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESorace201916-474] In 2021, Xi quoted the Yan'an Talks during the opening ceremony of the 11th National Congress of the [China Federation of Literary and Art Circles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Federation_of_Literary_and_Art_Circles) and the 10th National Congress of the [Chinese Writers Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Writers_Association).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquisQiao2022174-475] According to Xi, art should be judged by political criteria.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESorace201916-474] This view rejects the concept of [art-for-art's-sake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_for_art's_sake) and contends that art should serve the goal of national rejuvenation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESorace201916-474] Xi criticizes market-driven art which he deems sensationalist, particularly works which "exaggerate society's dark side" for profit.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESorace201916-474] He ordered the arts industry to "tell China's stories and spread Chinese voices to strengthen the country's international communication capacity."[^cite_note-476] Xi states that Chinese writers should follow the Party's leadership, serve the cause of socialism, and "let people see the good, feel hope, \[and] have dreams".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETu2025252-477] Xi is a proponent of the "[Sinicization of Chinese religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_policy_of_Xi_Jinping)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMariani2025288-478] At the [19th Party Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/19th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), Xi stated, "We will fully implement the Party's basic policy on religious affairs, uphold the principle that religions in China must be Chinese in orientation and provide active guidance to religions so that they can adapt themselves to a socialist society."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMariani2025288-478]

### Ideology

See also: [Ideology of the Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party)

Xi Jinping delivering a speech on 30 June 2021 at the [100th anniversary of the CCP's founding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100th_Anniversary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party)

Xi has said that "only socialism can save China."[^cite_note-479] Xi has also declared [socialism with Chinese characteristics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_with_Chinese_characteristics) to be the "only correct path to realize national rejuvenation."[^cite_note-480] According to [BBC News](1139893.md), while the CCP was perceived to have abandoned its communist ideology since it initiated [economic reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up) in the 1970s, Xi is believed by some observers to be more believing in the "idea of a communist project",[^cite_note-481] and was described as a [Marxist–Leninist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxist%E2%80%93Leninist) by former Australian prime minister Kevin Rudd.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERudd202435–36-482] Xi's emphasis on prioritizing ideology has included re-asserting the Party's goal of eventually [realizing communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_mode_of_production) and reprimanding those who dismiss communism as impractical or irrelevant.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiekeHofman202248-231] Xi described the communist ideal as the "calcium" in a Party member's spine, without which the Party member would suffer the "osteoporosis" of political decay and be unable to stand upright.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPiekeHofman202248-231]

Xi supports stronger Party leadership, saying "government, the military, society and schools, north, south, east and west – [the party leads them all](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Party_leads_everything)."[^cite_note-483] During the [100th anniversary of the CCP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100th_Anniversary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) in 2021, he said that "[without the Communist Party of China, there would be no new China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Without_the_Communist_Party,_There_Would_Be_No_New_China) and no national rejuvenation," and that "the leadership of the Party is the defining feature of socialism with Chinese characteristics and constitutes the greatest strength of this system."[^cite_note-484] Xi has said that China, despite many setbacks, has achieved great progress under the CCP, saying that "socialism with Chinese characteristics has become the standard-bearer of 21st-century socialist development."[^cite_note-\:12bb-485] However, he also warned that it will take a long time for China under the CCP to complete its rejuvenation, and during this timeframe, party members must be vigilant to not let CCP rule collapse.[^cite_note-\:12bb-485] For the CCP to maintain "purity" and "eternal youth" and not led Party rule to collapse, Xi has called on cadres to engage in [self-revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-revolution), through which the CCP roots out corruption and ineffectual officials.[^cite_note-\:010-486] Xi has said that "our party is so large, and our country, so huge–it is such that if the Party Central lacks the sole authority to make decisions, nothing can be achieved", comparing the Party Central to the CCP's "[cerebrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerebrum)" and the "[central nervous system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_nervous_system)", and said it "should have the sole authority in making decisions".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETsangCheung202470-487]

Xi has spoken out against "[historical nihilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_nihilism)", meaning historical viewpoints that challenge the official line of the CCP.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWong2023181-488] Xi said that one of the reasons for the [collapse of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) has been historical nihilism.[^cite_note-489] Subscribing to the view that socialism will eventually triumph over capitalism, Xi has said "Marx and Engels's [analysis of the basic contradictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dialectical_materialism) of [capitalist society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalist_mode_of_production_\(Marxist_theory\)) is not outdated, nor is the [historical materialist view](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_materialism) that capitalism is bound to die out and socialism bound to win."[^cite_note-Hancock-2022-490] Xi has called for the further [Sinicization of Marxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinicization_of_Marxism), referring to adapting [Marxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism) to the Chinese context. In July 2021, he formulated the [Two Integrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Integrations), calling for integrating Marxism with China's specific conditions and China's traditional culture.[^cite_note-491] Xi has called on CCP members to "remain true to our original aspiration", meaning to work for the happiness and well-being of the Chinese people and the rejuvenation of China, and launched an [educational campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remain_true_to_our_original_aspiration_and_keep_our_mission_firmly_in_mind) for that goal from 2019 to 2020.[^cite_note-\:08-492] Xi has said that the "feeling of devotion and sense of attachment to our motherland is a duty and responsibility of every Chinese", while adding "In contemporary China, the essence of patriotism is loving the country, the Party and socialism all at the same time".[^cite_note-493]

> China's success proves that socialism is not dead. It is thriving. Just imagine this: had socialism failed in China, had our communist party collapsed like the party in the Soviet Union, then global socialism would lapse into a long dark age. And communism, like Karl Marx once said, would be a haunting spectre lingering in limbo.

— Xi Jinping during a speech in 2018

[^cite_note-\:12bb-485]

Xi has ruled out a [multi-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system) for China, saying that "constitutional monarchy, imperial restoration, parliamentarism, a multi-party system and a presidential system, we considered them, tried them, but none worked."[^cite_note-494] However, Xi considers China to be a [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_China), saying that "China's [socialist democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_democracy) is the most comprehensive, genuine and effective democracy."[^cite_note-Drinhausen-2009-495] China's definition of democracy is different from [liberal democracies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracies) and is rooted in Marxism–Leninism, and is based on the phrases [people's democratic dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_democratic_dictatorship) and [democratic centralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_centralism).[^cite_note-Drinhausen-2009-495] Xi has additionally coined the term whole-process people's democracy which he said was about having "the people as masters".[^cite_note-496] Foreign analysts and observers have widely disputed that China is a democracy, saying that it is a one-party authoritarian state and Xi an authoritarian leader.[^cite_note-dictatorandauthoritarian-501] Xi has sometimes referred to as "[traditionalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_conservatism)" or "[neo-authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_China#Neoauthoritarianism)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFenby201932-502][^cite_note-\:1-468][^cite_note-\:12-308] Xi has additionally rejected [Westernisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westernization) as the only way to modernize, instead promoting what he says is [Chinese modernization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_modernization).[^cite_note-503]

### Xi Jinping Thought

Main article: [Xi Jinping Thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought)



A billboard promoting [Xi Jinping Thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping_Thought) in [Shenzhen](83368.md)

In January 2013, Politburo Standing Committee member [Liu Yunshan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liu_Yunshan) referred to Xi's speech for the [18th Party National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18th_National_Congress_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) as the "General Secretary Xi Jinping's Series of Important Speeches". In October 2017, the CCP Central Committee announced that Xi's political philosophies were developed into the "Xi Jinping Thought on Socialism with Chinese Characteristics for a New Era".[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505] Xi first made mention of the "Thought on Socialism with Chinese Characteristics for a New Era" in his opening day speech delivered to the 19th Party Congress in October 2017. His Politburo Standing Committee colleagues, in their own reviews of Xi's keynote address at the Congress, prepended the name "Xi Jinping" in front of "Thought".[^cite_note-xithought2-506] On 24 October 2017, at its closing session, the 19th Party Congress approved the incorporation of Xi Jinping Thought into the Constitution of the CCP,[^cite_note-PhillipsGuardian2-507] while in March 2018, the National People's Congress amended the state constitution to include Xi Jinping Thought.[^cite_note-508]



*[The Governance of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Governance_of_China)* in different languages presented at [Shanghai Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Library)

Xi himself has described the Thought as part of the broad framework created around socialism with Chinese characteristics, a term coined by Deng Xiaoping that places China in the [primary stage of socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_stage_of_socialism). The thought's main elements are summarized in the ten affirmations, the fourteen commitments, and the thirteen areas of achievements.[^cite_note-509] In official party documentation and pronouncements by Xi's colleagues, the Thought is said to be a continuation of [Marxism–Leninism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism%E2%80%93Leninism), [Mao Zedong Thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong_Thought), [Deng Xiaoping Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping_Theory), the [Three Represents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Represents), and the [Scientific Outlook on Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Outlook_on_Development), as part of a series of guiding ideologies that embody "Marxism adopted to Chinese conditions" and contemporary considerations.[^cite_note-xithought2-506] It has additionally been described as the "21st century Marxism" by two professors in the Central Party School of the CCP.[^cite_note-Page-2020-19] [Wang Huning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Huning), a top political adviser and a close ally of Xi, has been described as pivotal to developing Xi Jinping Thought.[^cite_note-Page-2020-19] The concepts and context behind Xi Jinping Thought are elaborated in Xi's *[The Governance of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Governance_of_China)* book series, published by the [Foreign Languages Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Languages_Press) for an international audience. Volume one was published in September 2014, followed by volume two in November 2017.[^cite_note-510] *[Xuexi Qiangguo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xuexi_Qiangguo)*, an app for teaching Xi Jinping Thought had become the most popular smartphone app in China in 2019, as the CCP launched a new campaign that calls on its cadres to immerse themselves in the political doctrine every day.[^cite_note-511]

## Personal life

### Family

Main article: [Xi family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_family)



Xi and his wife [Peng Liyuan](10462776.md) landing in [Bali](4147.md) to attend the [APEC Indonesia 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APEC_Indonesia_2013) in 2013

Xi's first marriage was to [Ke Lingling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ke_Lingling), the daughter of [Ke Hua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ke_Hua), China's ambassador to the United Kingdom in the early 1980s. They divorced within a few years.[^cite_note-512] The two were said to fight "almost every day," and after the divorce, Ke moved to the UK.[^cite_note-newyorker2-17] In 1987, Xi married famed folk singer [Peng Liyuan](10462776.md).[^cite_note-513] Xi and Peng were introduced by friends as many Chinese couples were in the 1980s.[^cite_note-514] Peng had gained fame in the 1980s and remained far better known in China than Xi until the latter emerged as a likely successor in the late 2000s. The couple frequently lived apart due largely to their separate professional lives. Peng has played a much more visible role as China's "first lady" compared to her predecessors; for example, Peng hosted U.S. first lady [Michelle Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Obama) on her high-profile visit to China in March 2014.[^cite_note-515] Xi and Peng have a daughter named [Xi Mingze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Mingze), who graduated from [Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University) in the spring of 2015. While at Harvard, she used a pseudonym and studied Psychology and English.[^cite_note-516] Xi's family has a home in [Jade Spring Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jade_Spring_Hill), a garden and residential area in north-western Beijing run by the CMC.[^cite_note-517]

In June 2012, [Bloomberg News](3057255.md) reported that members of Xi's extended family including his sister [Qi Qiaoqiao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qi_Qiaoqiao) have substantial business interests, although there was no evidence he had intervened to assist them.[^cite_note-518] The Bloomberg News website was blocked in [mainland China](55079.md) in response to the article about his family's wealth and the sales of [Bloomberg Terminals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomberg_Terminal) in China slowed,[^cite_note-519] while access to the files of Qinchuan Dadi, which was Qi's investment vehicle, was shut down.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESheridan2024153-520] Relatives of highly placed Chinese officials, including seven current and former members of the Politburo, have been named in the [Panama Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Papers), including [Deng Jiagui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Jiagui),[^cite_note-521] Xi's brother-in-law. Deng had two [shell companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shell_corporation) in the [British Virgin Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Virgin_Islands) while Xi was a member of the Politburo Standing Committee, which became dormant by the time Xi became CCP general secretary in 2012.[^cite_note-522] According to *[The New York Times](30680.md)* in 2012, after coming to power, Xi told his family to get out of their investments and that Qi Qiaoqiao and Deng Jiagui had divested their investments in at least 10 companies.[^cite_note-523] In 2015, [Wang Jianlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Jianlin), the leader of the company [Dalian Wanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wanda_Group), defended Qi and Deng, saying that they sold their shares in the company two months before the IPO in October 2014. *The New York Times* commented that this information is conflicted with official documents, which state that Qi and Deng transferred the ownership of the holding company to one of their employees in October 2013.[^cite_note-524]

### Personality

Peng described Xi as hardworking and down-to-earth: "When he comes home, I've never felt as if there's some leader in the house. In my eyes, he's just my husband."[^cite_note-525] In 1992, *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* journalist Lena H. Sun had an interview with Xi, then CCP secretary of Fuzhou; Sun described Xi as considerably more at ease and confident than many officials his age, and said that he talked without consulting notes.[^cite_note-526] He was described in a 2011 *The Washington Post* article by those who know him as "pragmatic, serious, cautious, hard-working, down to earth and low-key." He was described as a good hand at problem solving and "seemingly uninterested in the trappings of high office."[^cite_note-527] The Chinese state media has also cast him as a fatherly figure and a man of the people, determined to stand up for Chinese interests.[^cite_note-Myers-2018-455]

### Interests

Unlike previous Chinese leaders, Chinese state media has given a more encompassing view of Xi's private life, although still strictly controlled. According to Xinhua News Agency, Xi would swim one kilometer and walk every day as long as there was time, and is interested in foreign writers, especially Russian.[^cite_note-Blachard-2017-457] Some of his favorite foreign authors include [Leo Tolstoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Tolstoy), [Mikhail Sholokhov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Sholokhov), [Victor Hugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Hugo), [Honoré de Balzac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honor%C3%A9_de_Balzac), [Johann Wolfgang von Goethe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Wolfgang_von_Goethe) and [Jack London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_London).[^cite_note-528] Xi reportedly invoked *[What Is to Be Done?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/What_Is_to_Be_Done%3F_\(novel\))* by [Nikolay Chernyshevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Chernyshevsky) as a guide during the [16th BRICS summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_BRICS_summit).[^cite_note-529] Xi is reported to also like films and TV shows such as *[Saving Private Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saving_Private_Ryan)*,[^cite_note-530] *[Sleepless in Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleepless_in_Seattle)*, *[The Godfather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Godfather)*,[^cite_note-531] and *[Game of Thrones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_of_Thrones)*,[^cite_note-532] and he has praised Chinese independent film-maker [Jia Zhangke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jia_Zhangke).[^cite_note-533] Xi is also known to be an avid football fan and gave impetus to the [Great Chinese Football Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Chinese_Football_Dream) after stating that he had three wishes for the [China national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_national_football_team): to qualify for a [World Cup](11370.md), host a World Cup and win a World Cup by 2050.[^cite_note-534][^cite_note-soccer-535]

## Public image

It is hard to gauge the opinion of the Chinese public on Xi, as no independent surveys exist in China and mentions of his name in social media are heavily censored.[^cite_note-536] However, he is believed to be widely popular in the country.[^cite_note-537][^cite_note-538] According to a 2014 poll co-sponsored by the [Harvard Kennedy School's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_School_of_Government) [Ash Center for Democratic Governance and Innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash_Center_for_Democratic_Governance_and_Innovation), Xi ranked 9 out of 10 in domestic approval ratings.[^cite_note-539] A [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) poll released in July 2019 found that about 22% of people in mainland China list Xi as the person they admire the most, a plurality, although this figure was less than 5% for residents of Hong Kong.[^cite_note-540]

In the spring of 2026, the Pew Research Center made a survey on confidence on Xi among 36 countries, which indicated that a median 34% have confidence in Xi to do the right thing regarding world affairs, meanwhile a median of 53% have no confidence; these numbers are higher than those of Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) (31% confidence, 65% no confidence) and United States President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) (23% confidence, 76% no confidence).[^cite_note-541]

In 2017, *The Economist* named him the most powerful person in the world.[^cite_note-542] In 2018, *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* ranked him as [the most powerful and influential person in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_list_of_the_World's_Most_Powerful_People), replacing Russian President Vladimir Putin, who had been ranked so for five consecutive years.[^cite_note-543] In 2022, *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* also named Xi as the world's most powerful person, writing Xi "is the first among 1.4 billion people. If not among 7.7 billion."[^cite_note-544] Since 2013, [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md), an international non-profit and non-governmental organization with the stated aim of safeguarding the right to freedom of information, included Xi among the list of [press freedom predators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_freedom_predator).[^cite_note-545]

## Honours

Main article: [List of awards and honours received by Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_awards_and_honours_received_by_Xi_Jinping)

Xi has received state honours from several countries, including the [Order of the Golden Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Golden_Eagle) from Kazakhstan and the [Order of the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orders,_decorations,_and_medals_of_Tajikistan) from Tajikistan.[^cite_note-546][^cite_note-547]
